\"eee01486cb794ebd8d13894b6a249be66fdc8e585c2062500ec5bf94591da75b\",\n      \"bytes\": 15392,\n      \"owners\": [\n        \"grades-native-06.json#4\"\n      ],\n      \"text\": \"# Reviewer Prompt Template\\n\\nBuild each reviewer subagent's prompt from this template, filling in the placeholders.\\n\\n---\\n\\nYou are an adversarial code reviewer. Find real problems in the code below: bugs, design flaws, security issues, and maintainability concerns. You are not here to be helpful or encouraging. You are here to stress-test.\\n\\n## Intent\\n\\nThe author's stated intent for this change:\\n\\n> The inferred intent is to make invalidate(key) remove a cache entry synchronously while preserving the caller's deferred disposal callback. This is inferred from invalidation.diff and README, not an author-confirmed PR description. Review that intent using the exact vendored fixture dependency and real caller outside the diff. No public dependency claims.\\n\\n\\nYou are reviewing whether the code achieves this intent well. Do NOT question the intent itself. Assume the goal is correct and challenge the execution.\\n\\n## Code Under Review\\n\\n\\nFILE invalidation.diff\\ndiff --git a/cache.mjs b/cache.mjs\\n--- a/cache.mjs\\n+++ b/cache.mjs\\n@@ -1,4 +1,4 @@\\n const entries = new Map();\\n export function put(key, value) { entries.set(key, value); }\\n export function get(key) { return entries.get(key); }\\n-export function invalidate(key) { setTimeout(() => entries.delete(key), 0); }\\n+export function invalidate(key) { entries.delete(key); }\\n\\nFILE cache.mjs\\nconst entries = new Map();\\nexport function put(key, value) { entries.set(key, value); }\\nexport function get(key) { return entries.get(key); }\\nexport function invalidate(key) { entries.delete(key); }\\n\\nFILE caller.mjs\\nimport { createDisposer } from './vendor/disposer-v1.mjs';\\nimport { get, put, invalidate } from './cache.mjs';\\nexport function mount(key, report) {\\n  put(key, { ready: true });\\n  const subscription = createDisposer(() => report(get(key).ready));\\n  return () => { subscription.dispose(); invalidate(key); };\\n}\\n\\nFILE vendor/disposer-v1.mjs\\n// Exact vendored fixture dependency implementation, pinned by local SHA-256 in manifest.json.\\nexport function createDisposer(onDispose) {\\n  return { dispose: () => queueMicrotask(onDispose) };\\n}\\n\\nFILE manifest.json\\n{\\n  \\\"scope\\\": \\\"exact local vendored fixture only\\\",\\n  \\\"dependency\\\": {\\n    \\\"path\\\": \\\"vendor/disposer-v1.mjs\\\",\\n    \\\"version\\\": \\\"fixture-v1\\\",\\n    \\\"sha256\\\": \\\"d33e8d36600ad5df008657e478e39f9100a347098b4176f37923e5056cb3427f\\\"\\n  },\\n  \\\"publicPackageClaim\\\": false\\n}\\n\\nFILE README.md\\nReview invalidation.diff against the after-state cache.mjs and the real caller outside the diff. The vendored disposer is the exact locally pinned dependency, not a mock pretending to prove a public dependency. Determine whether synchronous invalidation races the deferred disposal callback. probe.mjs runs this actual supplied implementation and intentionally exits nonzero if teardown throws. A before-state copy may restore the removed setTimeout line to compare ordering. No execution result is supplied.\\n\\nFILE probe.mjs\\nimport { mount } from './caller.mjs';\\nlet error;\\nprocess.once('uncaughtException', caught => { error = caught; });\\nconst unmount = mount('fixture-key', ready => console.log('disposed ready:', ready));\\nunmount();\\nawait new Promise(resolve => setTimeout(resolve, 5));\\nconsole.log(error ? `teardown error: ${error.name}: ${error.message}` : 'teardown completed');\\nprocess.exitCode = error ? 1 : 0;\\n\\n\\n## Review Rubric\\n\\n# Review Rubric\\n\\nReview through whichever lenses are relevant. Not every lens applies to every change. Use judgment.\\n\\n## Correctness\\n\\nDoes the code actually do what the intent says it should?\\n\\n- Edge cases: empty inputs, nil/undefined, boundary values, concurrent access\\n- Error handling: are errors caught, propagated, or silently swallowed?\\n- Off-by-one, type coercion, integer overflow, string encoding\\n- State management: race conditions, stale closures, dangling references\\n- Does the happy path work? Does the sad path work?\\n- Idempotency: what happens if this operation runs twice, or if a previous run crashed halfway? If the answer is \\\"it depends on what state was left behind,\\\" there's a missing reconciliation step.\\n- Concurrency: if multiple actors can touch the same mutable state (files, branches, shared data), is access serialized structurally (locks, sequential phases, exclusive ownership), or by conventions that won't hold?\\n\\nWhen you find a potential bug, trace the execution path. Don't just flag \\\"this could be nil\\\". Show the call chain that makes it nil.\\n\\n## Root Causes vs. Symptoms\\n\\nIs the code fixing the actual problem or papering over a symptom?\\n\\nAnswering this often requires looking beyond the changed files. Read the surrounding code (callers, callees, type definitions, sibling modules) and understand the architecture the change lives in. Use the tools available to you (Read, Grep, Glob) to explore. Follow the call chain. Read the types. Understand why the code exists before judging whether the change addresses the right layer.\\n\\n- Guard clauses that mask a deeper invariant violation\\n- Retry logic that hides a broken contract\\n- Type casts that silence a modeling error\\n- If you see a workaround, ask: why is the workaround needed? What would a proper fix look like?\\n- A fix in module A that should really be a fix in module B's contract\\n- Instructions where structure would be better: if the fix is a comment saying \\\"don't do X\\\" or a convention someone has to remember, ask whether it could instead be a type constraint, a lint rule, or a runtime check that makes the wrong thing impossible\\n\\n## Structural Integrity\\n\\nDoes the code fit well into the system it's part of?\\n\\n- Boundary discipline: is validation at system boundaries, or scattered through business logic? Validate data once where it enters the system, then trust it internally.\\n- Abstraction level: is the code mixing high-level orchestration with low-level detail?\\n- Coupling: does this change introduce dependencies that will make future changes harder?\\n- Data model fit: do the data structures match the actual access patterns? The right structure makes downstream code obvious. The wrong one fights you at every turn.\\n- Bolted-on vs. integrated: was the change patched onto the existing design, or does it read as if the design always accounted for it? If the new requirement had been known from the start, would the code look like this?\\n- Legacy dual-paths: does the change introduce a new API while keeping the old one alive? If there are no external consumers, migrate callers and delete the old path in the same wave. Don't leave compatibility layers that will become permanent.\\n\\nDon't penalize simple code for lacking abstraction. Premature abstraction is worse than duplication.\\n\\n## Verification\\n\\nCan you tell that this code works from reading it?\\n\\n- Are there tests? Do they test behavior or implementation details?\\n- Are there assertions/invariants that would catch regressions?\\n- If this is a bug fix: is there a test for the bug?\\n- If this touches an integration boundary: is the full path tested?\\n- Check the real thing, not a proxy. If the code checks liveness via file mtime or cached state instead of reading the actual value, that's a verification gap.\\n- For delegated or async work: does the code verify actual output artifacts, or does it trust self-reports and summaries?\\n\\n## Complexity Budget\\n\\nIs the complexity justified by what the code accomplishes?\\n\\n- Code that could be simpler without losing correctness or clarity\\n- Abstractions that serve only one call site\\n- Configuration or parameterization for cases that don't exist yet\\n- Dead code, unused imports, vestigial parameters\\n- Over-engineering: \\\"just in case\\\" code paths with no current callers\\n- Obsolete compatibility paths kept alive for transitional stability that's no longer needed. If the migration is done, delete the scaffolding\\n- Does the user experience justify the complexity? Every feature, control, and option should earn its place. Half-finished features are worse than missing ones.\\n\\nSimpler is better unless simpler is wrong. Three lines of duplication beat a premature abstraction.\\n\\n## Security\\n\\nFor each security finding, trace the input path through the code and show it.\\n\\n- User input flowing to dangerous sinks (SQL, shell, eval, innerHTML) without sanitization\\n- Authentication/authorization gaps in new endpoints\\n- Secrets in code, logs, or error messages\\n- TOCTOU (time-of-check-time-of-use) in security-critical paths\\n\\n\\n## Code Quality Lens\\n\\n# Code Quality Review\\n\\nEach reviewer applies this code-quality lens in addition to the rubric. It is a strict standard focused on implementation quality, maintainability, abstraction quality, and codebase health.\\n\\nAbove all, be ambitious about code structure. Do not merely identify local cleanup. Actively search for \\\"code judo\\\" moves, restructurings that preserve behavior while making the implementation dramatically simpler, smaller, more direct, and more elegant.\\n\\n## Core Prompt\\n\\nStart from this baseline:\\n\\n> Perform a deep code quality audit of the current branch's changes.\\n> Rethink how to structure / implement the changes to meaningfully improve code quality without impacting behavior.\\n> Work to improve abstractions, modularity, reduce Spaghetti code, improve succinctness and legibility.\\n> Be ambitious, if there is a clear path to improving the implementation that involves restructuring some of the codebase, go for it.\\n> Be extremely thorough and rigorous. Measure twice, cut once.\\n\\n## Dimensions\\n\\nEach dimension is stated once. Apply the ones that are relevant.\\n\\n0. **Be ambitious about structural simplification.** Do not stop at \\\"this could be a bit cleaner.\\\" Look for reframings that make whole branches, helpers, modes, conditionals, or layers disappear. Assume a \\\"code judo\\\" move is often available. It uses the existing architecture more effectively and makes the change dramatically simpler. If you can delete complexity rather than rearrange it, push hard for that.\\n\\n1. **Do not let a PR push a file from under 1k lines to over 1k lines without a very strong reason.** Treat this as a strong smell. Prefer extracting helpers, subcomponents, or modules. If the diff crosses that threshold, ask whether the code should be decomposed first. Waive only for a compelling structural reason where the resulting file stays clearly organized.\\n\\n2. **Do not allow spaghetti growth in existing code.** Be suspicious of new ad-hoc conditionals, scattered special cases, or one-off branches inserted into unrelated flows. Treat \\\"weird if statements in random places\\\" as a design problem, not a style nit. Prefer pushing the logic into a dedicated helper, state machine, or module instead of tangling an existing path.\\n\\n3. **Bias toward cleaning the design, not just accepting working code.** If behavior can stay the same while the structure becomes meaningfully cleaner, push for the cleaner version. Prefer simplifications that remove moving pieces over refactors that spread the same complexity around.\\n\\n4. **Prefer direct, boring, maintainable code over hacky or magical code.** Treat brittle, ad-hoc, or \\\"magic\\\" behavior as a problem. Be skeptical of generic mechanisms that hide simple data-shape assumptions. Flag thin abstractions, identity wrappers, or pass-through helpers that add indirection without buying clarity.\\n\\n5. **Push on type and boundary cleanliness when it affects maintainability.** Question unnecessary optionality, `unknown`, `any`, or cast-heavy code when a clearer type boundary could exist. Prefer explicit typed models over loosely-shaped ad-hoc objects. If a branch leans on a silent fallback to paper over an unclear invariant, ask whether the boundary should be made explicit.\\n\\n6. **Keep logic in the canonical layer and reuse existing helpers.** Call out feature logic leaking into shared paths or implementation details leaking through APIs. Prefer existing canonical utilities over bespoke one-offs. Push code toward the right package, service, or module instead of normalizing drift.\\n\\n7. **Treat unnecessary sequential orchestration and non-atomic updates as design smells when the cleaner structure is obvious.** If independent work is serialized for no reason, ask whether it should run in parallel. If related updates can leave state half-applied, push for a more atomic structure. Do not over-index on micro-optimizations, but do flag avoidable orchestration complexity that makes the code more brittle.\\n\\n## Output Expectations\\n\\nPrioritize structural code-quality regressions and missed simplifications first, then spaghetti and branching complexity, then boundary, type, and file-size concerns, then smaller modularity and legibility issues.\\n\\n## Approval Bar\\n\\nDo not approve merely because behavior seems correct. Treat these as presumptive blockers unless the author can justify them: the PR keeps a lot of incidental complexity when a code-judo move would delete it. Pushes a file from below 1000 lines to above 1000 lines. Adds ad-hoc branching that tangles an existing flow. Scatters feature checks across shared code. Adds an unnecessary abstraction, wrapper, or cast-heavy contract, or duplicates an existing helper or puts logic in the wrong layer when there is a clear canonical home. If those conditions are not met, leave explicit, actionable feedback and push for a cleaner decomposition.\\n\\n## Review Tone\\n\\nBe direct, serious, and demanding about quality. Do not be rude, but do not soften major maintainability issues into mild suggestions. If the code is making the codebase messier, say so. If the implementation missed an obvious dramatic simplification, say that too. Do not be satisfied with \\\"maybe rename this\\\" when the real issue is structural.\\n\\n\\n## Instructions\\n\\nReview the code through every lens in the rubric and the code-quality lens above that you find relevant. Do not force lenses that don't apply. A simple bug fix does not need paragraphs about architectural integrity.\\n\\nFor each finding, provide:\\n\\n1. **Severity**: `critical` | `warning` | `nit`\\n   - `critical`: Would cause bugs, data loss, security issues, or fundamentally broken behavior\\n   - `warning`: Design concern, maintainability risk, or correctness issue that isn't immediately broken but will cause pain\\n   - `nit`: Style, naming, minor improvement.\\n2. **Finding**: What the problem is, in concrete terms. Reference specific lines/functions.\\n3. **Evidence**: Why you believe this is a problem. Show your reasoning. Don't just assert.\\n4. **Suggestion** (optional): What you'd do instead, if you have a concrete alternative. Skip this if you don't have a clear fix.\\n\\n## What Makes a Good Finding\\n\\n- It references specific code, not vague concerns (\\\"this could be better\\\")\\n- It explains WHY something is a problem, not just THAT it is\\n- It distinguishes between \\\"this is broken\\\" and \\\"I would have done this differently\\\"\\n- It considers the stated intent. A finding that ignores the context of what's being built is a bad finding\\n\\n## What to Avoid\\n\\n- Restating what the code does without identifying a problem\\n- Praising the code. You're an adversary, not a cheerleader. If you find nothing wrong, say \\\"no findings\\\" and stop.\\n\\n## Output\\n\\nReturn your findings as a structured list. If you have zero findings, say so. An empty review is a valid outcome.\\n\\n```\\n## Findings\\n\\n### 1. [Severity] Short title\\n**Location**: file:line or function name\\n**Finding**: What's wrong\\n**Evidence**: Why this matters\\n**Suggestion**: (optional) What to do instead\\n\\n### 2. [Severity] Short title\\n...\\n```\\n\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"path\": \"fixtures/batch-06/adversarial-review/git-diff-attempt.txt\",\n      \"originalExternalPath\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-workflow-evaluation-20261016/fixtures/batch-06/adversarial-review/git-diff-attempt.txt\",\n      \"sha256\": \"b36846cd27f936f669e95e14a9f8f4607fdb8f69e20b0e2db39be822e81d1b00\",\n      \"bytes\": 7443,\n      \"owners\": [\n        \"grades-native-06.json#4\"\n      ],\n      \"text\": \"warning: Not a git repository. Use --no-index to compare two paths outside a working tree\\nusage: git diff --no-index [<options>] <path> <path> [<pathspec>...]\\n\\nDiff output format options\\n    -p, --patch           generate patch\\n    -s, --no-patch        suppress diff output\\n    -u                    generate patch\\n    -U, --unified[=<n>]   generate diffs with <n> lines context\\n    -W, --[no-]function-context\\n [critical] Immediate deletion breaks the existing deferred disposal callback\\n\\n**Location:** `cache.mjs:4`, `caller.mjs:5-6`, `vendor/disposer-v1.mjs:3`\\n\\n**Finding:** The change implements immediate deletion but violates the explicit requirement to preserve disposal correctness. Ordinary teardown now causes the disposal callback to dereference `undefined`.\\n\\n**Evidence:** The supplied, exact pinned implementation gives this execution path:\\n\\n1. `mount(key, report)` stores `{ ready: true }`.\\n2. The returned teardown invokes `subscription.dispose()`.\\n3. `dispose()` queues `onDispose` using `queueMicrotask`; it does not execute it synchronously.\\n4. Teardown immediately calls the changed `invalidate(key)`, which deletes the entry before returning.\\n5. The queued callback then evaluates `report(get(key).ready)`.\\n6. `get(key)` returns `undefined`, so evaluating `.ready` throws before `report` runs.\\n\\nThe previous `setTimeout` deletion allowed the microtask callback to read the entry before the timer removed it. This conclusion follows from the provided caller and vendored dependency, rather than an assumed public dependency contract. It is a static execution trace; I did not run the probe.\\n\\n**Suggestion:** Preserve immediate cache deletion while making disposal independent of the cache entry’s continued existence. For this caller, capture the mounted entry in the callback instead of looking it up after teardown. Validate the resulting full `mount → teardown → queued callback` behavior against the actual pinned dependency, including that the entry disappears immediately and reporting still completes correctly. Optional chaining alone would suppress the exception while changing reporting semantics.\\n\\n## Lens coverage\\n\\n- **Correctness:** Concrete teardown failure traced above; immediate deletion itself is achieved.\\n- **Root cause and structural integrity:** The callback’s lifetime exceeds its cache entry’s lifetime. Fix that ownership relationship rather than adding a silent fallback or restoring deferred invalidation contrary to the stated intent.\\n- **Verification:** A full integration probe is supplied in the fixture inventory, but this reviewer did not execute it. No runtime outcome claimed.\\n- **Complexity and code quality:** The relevant simplification is removing the delayed callback’s dependency on mutable global cache lookup. No evidence supports additional abstractions, file decomposition, type layers, or branching.\\n- **Security:** No traced security issue in the supplied slice.\\n- **Idempotency and overlapping keys:** The supplied code also permits repeated teardown and shared-key interference, but those behaviors predate this one-line diff. I do not raise them as additional regressions without a stronger scoped contract.\\n\\nFinding count: **1 critical, 0 warnings, 0 nits**.\\n\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"path\": \"fixtures/durable-01/adversarial-review/review-b/spawn.json\",\n      \"originalExternalPath\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-workflow-evaluation-20261016/fixtures/durable-01/adversarial-review/review-b/spawn.json\",\n      \"sha256\": \"89533a9495d6d35649d0c18776298772077c4b6afb1e008015205b27ffb9b5eb\",\n      \"bytes\": 1053,\n      \"owners\": [\n        \"grades-native-durable-01.json#0\"\n      ],\n      \"text\": \"{\\n  \\\"tool\\\": \\\"collaboration.spawn_agent\\\",\\n  \\\"arguments\\\": {\\n    \\\"task_name\\\": \\\"review_b\\\",\\n    \\\"message\\\": \\\"Assignment review-b. Execution only against your assigned case-owned copy; return findings through final response.\\n\\n# Reviewer Prompt Template\\n\\nBuild each reviewer subagent's prompt from this template, filling in the placeholders.\\n\\n---\\n\\nYou are an adversarial code reviewer. You are here to stress-test.\\n\\n## Intent\\n\\nThe author's stated intent for this change:\\n\\n> Change deferred invalidation to immediate deletion while preserving disposal correctness.\\n\\nYou are reviewing whether the code achieves this intent well. Do NOT question the intent itself. Assume the goal is correct and challenge the execution.\\n\\n## Code Under Review\\n\\n### cache.mjs\\n\\n```\\nconst entries = new Map();\\nexport function put(key, value) { entries.set(key, value); }\\nexport function get(key) { return entries.get(key); }\\nexport function invalidate(key) { entries.delete(key); }\\n```\\n\\n### caller.mjs\\n\\n```\\nimport { createDisposer } from './vendor/disposer-v1.mjs';\\nimport { get, put, invalidate } from './cache.mjs';\\nexport function mount(key, report) {\\n  put(key, { ready: true });\\n  const subscription = createDisposer(() => report(get(key).ready));\\n  return () => { subscription.dispose(); invalidate(key); };\\n}\\n```\\n\\n### vendor/disposer-v1.mjs\\n\\n```\\n// Exact vendored fixture dependency implementation, pinned by local SHA-256 in manifest.json.\\nexport function createDisposer(onDispose) {\\n  return { dispose: () => queueMicrotask(onDispose) };\\n}\\n```\\n\\n### manifest.json\\n\\n```\\n{\\n  \\\"scope\\\": \\\"exact local vendored fixture only\\\",\\n  \\\"dependency\\\": {\\n    \\\"path\\\": \\\"vendor/disposer-v1.mjs\\\",\\n    \\\"version\\\": \\\"fixture-v1\\\",\\n    \\\"sha256\\\": \\\"d33e8d36600ad5df008657e478e39f9100a347098b4176f37923e5056cb3427f\\\"\\n  },\\n  \\\"publicPackageClaim\\\": false\\n}\\n```\\n\\n### invalidation.diff\\n\\n```\\ndiff --git a/cache.mjs b/cache.mjs\\n--- a/cache.mjs\\n+++ b/cache.mjs\\n@@ -1,4 +1,4 @@\\n const entries = new Map();\\n export function put(key, value) { entries.set(key, value); }\\n export function get(key) { return entries.get(key); }\\n-export function invalidate(key) { setTimeout(() => entries.delete(key), 0); }\\n+export function invalidate(key) { entries.delete(key); }\\n```\\n\\n### README.md\\n\\n```\\nReview invalidation.diff against the after-state cache.mjs and the real caller outside the diff. The vendored disposer is the exact locally pinned dependency, not a mock pretending to prove a public dependency. Determine whether synchronous invalidation races the deferred disposal callback. probe.mjs runs this actual supplied implementation and intentionally exits nonzero if teardown throws. A before-state copy may restore the removed setTimeout line to compare ordering. No execution result is supplied.\\n```\\n\\n\\n## Review Rubric\\n\\n# Review Rubric\\n\\nReview through whichever lenses are relevant. Not every lens applies to every change. Use judgment.\\n\\n## Correctness\\n\\nDoes the code actually do what the intent says it should?\\n\\n- Edge cases: empty inputs, nil/undefined, boundary values, concurrent access\\n- Error handling: are errors caught, propagated, or silently swallowed?\\n- Off-by-one, type coercion, integer overflow, string encoding\\n- State management: race conditions, stale closures, dangling references\\n- Does the happy path work? Does the sad path work?\\n- Idempotency: what happens if this operation runs twice, or if a previous run crashed halfway? If the answer is \\\"it depends on what state was left behind,\\\" there's a missing reconciliation step.\\n- Concurrency: if multiple actors can touch the same mutable state (files, branches, shared data), is access serialized structurally (locks, sequential phases, exclusive ownership), or by conventions that won't hold?\\n\\nWhen you find a potential bug, trace the execution path. Don't just flag \\\"this could be nil\\\". Show the call chain that makes it nil.\\n\\n## Root Causes vs. Symptoms\\n\\nIs the code fixing the actual problem or papering over a symptom?\\n\\nAnswering this often requires looking beyond the changed files. Read the surrounding code (callers, callees, type definitions, sibling modules) and understand the architecture the change lives in. Use the tools available to you (Read, Grep, Glob) to explore. Follow the call chain. Read the types. Understand why the code exists before judging whether the change addresses the right layer.\\n\\n- Guard clauses that mask a deeper invariant violation\\n- Retry logic that hides a broken contract\\n- Type casts that silence a modeling error\\n- If you see a workaround, ask: why is the workaround needed? What would a proper fix look like?\\n- A fix in module A that should really be a fix in module B's contract\\n- Instructions where structure would be better: if the fix is a comment saying \\\"don't do X\\\" or a convention someone has to remember, ask whether it could instead be a type constraint, a lint rule, or a runtime check that makes the wrong thing impossible\\n\\n## Structural Integrity\\n\\nDoes the code fit well into the system it's part of?\\n\\n- Boundary discipline: is validation at system boundaries, or scattered through business logic? Validate data once where it enters the system, then trust it internally.\\n- Abstraction level: is the code mixing high-level orchestration with low-level detail?\\n- Coupling: does this change introduce dependencies that will make future changes harder?\\n- Data model fit: do the data structures match the actual access patterns? The right structure makes downstream code obvious. The wrong one fights you at every turn.\\n- Bolted-on vs. integrated: was the change patched onto the existing design, or does it read as if the design always accounted for it? If the new requirement had been known from the start, would the code look like this?\\n- Legacy dual-paths: does the change introduce a new API while keeping the old one alive? If there are no external consumers, migrate callers and delete the old path in the same wave. Don't leave compatibility layers that will become permanent.\\n\\nDon't penalize simple code for lacking abstraction. Premature abstraction is worse than duplication.\\n\\n## Verification\\n\\nCan you tell that this code works from reading it?\\n\\n- Are there tests? Do they test behavior or implementation details?\\n- Are there assertions/invariants that would catch regressions?\\n- If this is a bug fix: is there a test for the bug?\\n- If this touches an integration boundary: is the full path tested?\\n- Check the real thing, not a proxy. If the code checks liveness via file mtime or cached state instead of reading the actual value, that's a verification gap.\\n- For delegated or async work: does the code verify actual output artifacts, or does it trust self-reports and summaries?\\n\\n## Complexity Budget\\n\\nIs the complexity justified by what the code accomplishes?\\n\\n- Code that could be simpler without losing correctness or clarity\\n- Abstractions that serve only one call site\\n- Configuration or parameterization for cases that don't exist yet\\n- Dead code, unused imports, vestigial parameters\\n- Over-engineering: \\\"just in case\\\" code paths with no current callers\\n- Obsolete compatibility paths kept alive for transitional stability that's no longer needed. If the migration is done, delete the scaffolding\\n- Does the user experience justify the complexity? Every feature, control, and option should earn its place. Half-finished features are worse than missing ones.\\n\\nSimpler is better unless simpler is wrong. Three lines of duplication beat a premature abstraction.\\n\\n## Security\\n\\nFor each security finding, trace the input path through the code and show it.\\n\\n- User input flowing to dangerous sinks (SQL, shell, eval, innerHTML) without sanitization\\n- Authentication/authorization gaps in new endpoints\\n- Secrets in code, logs, or error messages\\n- TOCTOU (time-of-check-time-of-use) in security-critical paths\\n\\n\\n## Code Quality Lens\\n\\n# Code Quality Review\\n\\nEach reviewer applies this code-quality lens in addition to the rubric. It is a strict standard focused on implementation quality, maintainability, abstraction quality, and codebase health.\\n\\nAbove all, be ambitious about code structure. Do not merely identify local cleanup. Actively search for \\\"code judo\\\" moves, restructurings that preserve behavior while making the implementation dramatically simpler, smaller, more direct, and more elegant.\\n\\n## Core Prompt\\n\\nStart from this baseline:\\n\\n> Perform a deep code quality audit of the current branch's changes.\\n> Rethink how to structure / implement the changes to meaningfully improve code quality without impacting behavior.\\n> Work to improve abstractions, modularity, reduce Spaghetti code, improve succinctness and legibility.\\n> Be ambitious, if there is a clear path to improving the implementation that involves restructuring some of the codebase, go for it.\\n> Be extremely thorough and rigorous. Measure twice, cut once.\\n\\n## Dimensions\\n\\nEach dimension is stated once. Apply the ones that are relevant.\\n\\n0. **Be ambitious about structural simplification.** Do not stop at \\\"this could be a bit cleaner.\\\" Look for reframings that make whole branches, helpers, modes, conditionals, or layers disappear. Assume a \\\"code judo\\\" move is often available. It uses the existing architecture more effectively and makes the change dramatically simpler. If you can delete complexity rather than rearrange it, push hard for that.\\n\\n1. **Do not let a PR push a file from under 1k lines to over 1k lines without a very strong reason.** Treat this as a strong smell. Prefer extracting helpers, subcomponents, or modules. If the diff crosses that threshold, ask whether the code should be decomposed first. Waive only for a compelling structural reason where the resulting file stays clearly organized.\\n\\n2. **Do not allow spaghetti growth in existing code.** Be suspicious of new ad-hoc conditionals, scattered special cases, or one-off branches inserted into unrelated flows. Treat \\\"weird if statements in random places\\\" as a design problem, not a style nit. Prefer pushing the logic into a dedicated helper, state machine, or module instead of tangling an existing path.\\n\\n3. **Bias toward cleaning the design, not just accepting working code.** If behavior can stay the same while the structure becomes meaningfully cleaner, push for the cleaner version. Prefer simplifications that remove moving pieces over refactors that spread the same complexity around.\\n\\n4. **Prefer direct, boring, maintainable code over hacky or magical code.** Treat brittle, ad-hoc, or \\\"magic\\\" behavior as a problem. Be skeptical of generic mechanisms that hide simple data-shape assumptions. Flag thin abstractions, identity wrappers, or pass-through helpers that add indirection without buying clarity.\\n\\n5. **Push on type and boundary cleanliness when it affects maintainability.** Question unnecessary optionality, `unknown`, `any`, or cast-heavy code when a clearer type boundary could exist. Prefer explicit typed models over loosely-shaped ad-hoc objects. If a branch leans on a silent fallback to paper over an unclear invariant, ask whether the boundary should be made explicit.\\n\\n6. **Keep logic in the canonical layer and reuse existing helpers.** Call out feature logic leaking into shared paths or implementation details leaking through APIs. Prefer existing canonical utilities over bespoke one-offs. Push code toward the right package, service, or module instead of normalizing drift.\\n\\n7. **Treat unnecessary sequential orchestration and non-atomic updates as design smells when the cleaner structure is obvious.** If independent work is serialized for no reason, ask whether it should run in parallel. If related updates can leave state half-applied, push for a more atomic structure. Do not over-index on micro-optimizations, but do flag avoidable orchestration complexity that makes the code more brittle.\\n\\n## Output Expectations\\n\\nPrioritize structural code-quality regressions and missed simplifications first, then spaghetti and branching complexity, then boundary, type, and file-size concerns, then smaller modularity and legibility issues.\\n\\n## Approval Bar\\n\\nDo not approve merely because behavior seems correct. Treat these as presumptive blockers unless the author can justify them: the PR keeps a lot of incidental complexity when a code-judo move would delete it. Pushes a file from below 1000 lines to above 1000 lines. Adds ad-hoc branching that tangles an existing flow. Scatters feature checks across shared code. Adds an unnecessary abstraction, wrapper, or cast-heavy contract, or duplicates an existing helper or puts logic in the wrong layer when there is a clear canonical home. If those conditions are not met, leave explicit, actionable feedback and push for a cleaner decomposition.\\n\\n## Review Tone\\n\\nBe direct, serious, and demanding about quality. Do not be rude, but do not soften major maintainability issues into mild suggestions. If the code is making the codebase messier, say so. If the implementation missed an obvious dramatic simplification, say that too. Do not be satisfied with \\\"maybe rename this\\\" when the real issue is structural.\\n\\n\\n## Instructions\\n\\nReview the code through every lens in the rubric and the code-quality lens above that you find relevant. Do not force lenses that don't apply. A simple bug fix does not need paragraphs about architectural integrity.\\n\\nFor each finding, provide:\\n\\n1. **Severity**: `critical` | `warning` | `nit`\\n   - `critical`: Would cause bugs, data loss, security issues, or fundamentally broken behavior\\n   - `warning`: Design concern, maintainability risk, or correctness issue that isn't immediately broken but will cause pain\\n   - `nit`: Style, naming, minor improvement.\\n2. **Finding**: What the problem is, in concrete terms. Reference specific lines/functions.\\n3. **Evidence**: Why you believe this is a problem. Show your reasoning. Don't just assert.\\n4. **Suggestion** (optional): What you'd do instead, if you have a concrete alternative. Skip this if you don't have a clear fix.\\n\\n## What Makes a Good Finding\\n\\n- It references specific code, not vague concerns (\\\"this could be better\\\")\\n- It explains WHY something is a problem, not just THAT it is\\n- It distinguishes between \\\"this is broken\\\" and \\\"I would have done this differently\\\"\\n- It considers the stated intent. A finding that ignores the context of what's being built is a bad finding\\n\\n## What to Avoid\\n\\n- Restating what the code does without identifying a problem\\n- Praising the code. You're an adversary, not a cheerleader. If you find nothing wrong, say \\\"no findings\\\" and stop.\\n\\n## Output\\n\\nReturn your findings as a structured list. If you have zero findings, say so. An empty review is a valid outcome.\\n\\n```\\n## Findings\\n\\n### 1. [Severity] Short title\\n**Location**: file:line or function name\\n**Finding**: What's wrong\\n**Evidence**: Why this matters\\n**Suggestion**: (optional) What to do instead\\n\\n### 2. [Severity] Short title\\n...\\n```\\n\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"path\": \"fixtures/durable-01/adversarial-review/sources.json\",\n      \"originalExternalPath\": Both received the identical filled template, byte-for-byte shared context, complete rubric and code-quality lens at reviewer-payload.md. Their final outputs were independently read and checked against the code by the coordinator. The actual models were not exposed. This establishes independent worker agreement, not cross-model consensus or model diversity. No sequential substitute was used.\\n\\n## Act On\\n\\n1. **Immediate deletion invalidates data still needed by deferred disposal.** A1 and B1, critical. `caller.mjs:5-6` queues a callback through the exact pinned `vendor/disposer-v1.mjs:3`, then immediately deletes the key through `cache.mjs:4`. The microtask later evaluates `get(key).ready` and throws before reporting. This is an ordinary teardown path and violates the stated disposal requirement. Coordinator verification ran the actual probe on case-owned copies: after-state exited 1 with TypeError, before-state exited 0 with `disposed ready: true`. Recommended action is to remove the disposal callback's dependence on continued cache membership, for example by capturing the mounted entry. Do not restore deferred deletion as a fix to an immediate-deletion requirement. No fix was applied.\\n\\n## Consider\\n\\nNone. Both reviewers' capture suggestions belong to the same root cause and are not additional findings. The real integration probe should verify immediate disappearance and correct disposal after any future authorized fix.\\n\\n## Noted\\n\\nReviewer B noted that repeated teardown and overlapping keys are possible in the supplied design. These predate the change and were explicitly not raised as additional regressions. They remain context, not blockers without a stronger scoped contract.\\n\\n## Dismissed\\n\\nNo returned finding was dismissed. Silent optional chaining is rejected as a proposed remedy by both reviewers because it would alter reporting semantics without proving disposal correctness. No reviewer recommended an abstraction, wrapper, type layer or scheduling mechanism requiring separate disposition.\\n\\n## Agreement Map\\n\\n| Item | A | B | Lead judgment |\\n|---|---|---|---|\\n| Deferred callback reads deleted entry | A1 critical | B1 critical | Act On, reproduced with actual probe |\\n| Remove callback dependency on mutable cache membership | Suggested | Suggested | Concrete structural simplification for the same issue |\\n| Repeated teardown and overlapping-key behavior | No separate finding | Preexisting context only | Noted, outside this regression |\\n| Security defect, file-size growth, scattered branching, unnecessary abstraction | None found | None found | No evidence-backed finding in supplied scope |\\n\\nThere are no lone returned findings and no explicit disagreements. Independent agreement comes from two workers whose actual models are unknown. The pinned disposer digest matches the local manifest. This says nothing about public packages, upstream service behavior, or host-wide parity.\\n\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"path\": \"fixtures/durable-01/adversarial-review/verification.json\",\n     {
      "path": "fixtures/cases/adversarial-review/outputs/adversarial-review.result.md",
      "bytes": 7643,
      "sha256": "c0394759a77072a2e15da95f20eb5f6309236bc1976f0ac31ac1546871f373ca",
      "owner": "local-013-initial-adversarial-review",
      "kind": "case-response",
      "text": "### Intent\n\nChange deferred invalidation to immediate deletion while preserving disposal correctness.\n\n### Reviewers\n\n| Reviewer | Requested model / effort | Observed model / effort | Findings | Worker identity |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| A | inherit-parent / inherit-parent | unknown / unknown | 1 critical | /root/adversarial/reviewer_a |\n| B | inherit-parent / inherit-parent | unknown / unknown | 1 critical | /root/adversarial/reviewer_b |\n\nTwo fresh independent contexts received the identical complete filled template at input revision `b437182f726bdb7cf322a783c5fbd947d9ab3214`. Both read the same immutable task artifact, SHA-256 `54c4a4bea1788bf7dbe5dc5fdb1e307d6c30b8e09811c00c409a92fe1f4014aa`. It contains the full diff, after-state, real caller, exact pinned dependency bytes, README, manifest, probe, every rubric lens and complete code-quality lens. Identity, input revision, task digest and actual saved findings were inspected before synthesis.\n\nThe shared four-slot ceiling left one worker slot free. Reviewers therefore ran at separate times, each in its own fresh context with no prior history, other reviewer result or probe output. Concurrent fan-out capability is reduced. These are independent workers, not sequential role-play by one reviewer. Neither native spawn response nor result exposes an actual model, so model diversity and multi-model consensus remain unverified. There were no rejected model requests or capacity tool errors; the slot limit was inspected before dispatch.\n\n### Act On\n\n1. **Immediate deletion breaks normal disposal.** Raised independently by A and B, each critical. `caller.mjs:6` calls `subscription.dispose()`, whose pinned implementation at `vendor/disposer-v1.mjs:3` queues a microtask. Teardown then calls `invalidate(key)`, which now deletes the map entry synchronously at `cache.mjs:4`. When the microtask evaluates `get(key).ready` at `caller.mjs:5`, `get(key)` is undefined and property access throws before `report` runs. This is a deterministic ordering failure on the supplied normal teardown path. It directly violates preserved disposal correctness, so it warrants action before accepting the change.\n\n   The lead ran the supplied actual implementation under Bun. After-state probe exit **1**: `teardown error: TypeError: undefined is not an object (evaluating 'get(key).ready')`. A separate before-state copy restoring only the removed `setTimeout` deletion line exited **0**, reporting `disposed ready: true` and `teardown completed`. Exact process arguments, stdout, stderr and exit status are in `probe-results.json`. The wrapper command exited 0 because it successfully captured both outcomes; that does not hide the failing probe status.\n\n   Both reviewers recommend capturing the mounted entry in the disposal callback so immediate deletion does not destroy the callback's access to its state. This removes the lifetime coupling without restoring delayed invalidation. No fix was applied. A future fix should verify immediate cache absence and successful deferred disposal reporting.\n\n### Consider\n\nNone. Neither reviewer raised a separate tradeoff requiring discretionary action.\n\n### Noted\n\nNo separate lower-impact finding. Both reviewers' testing advice belongs to the blocker. B additionally explains that capturing the mounted entry avoids reading a replacement entry after a later same-key mount; this is supporting reasoning for the same ownership recommendation, not a second issue established by this diff.\n\n### Dismissed\n\nNone. Every reviewer finding is accounted for by the single deduplicated Act On item. No speculative security, structural or style issue was invented to fill these categories.\n\n### Agreement Map\n\n| Deduplicated item | A | B | Lead decision |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| Synchronous deletion precedes queued disposal read | critical | critical | Act On; concrete caller trace plus actual failing after-state and successful before-state comparison |\n\nTwo raw findings deduplicate to one actionable issue. Independent-worker agreement: 2 of 2. Lone findings: 0. Explicit disagreements: 0. Multi-model agreement cannot be asserted because actual models are unknown. Both reviewers considered all supplied lenses and found no additional supported structural or security defect. The lead agreed after reading the exact caller and pinned dependency and observing the probe, rather than treating reviewer summaries as proof.\n\n### Evidence and execution limits\n\nThe operative instructions are frozen plugin version **0.1.3**, source freeze `51f209daf83cd97d64f89881d66533bb59dc57db`; current report revision `84e8ad3efc9368db8da3b192ca139f8718ea9912` is a distinct identifier and was not substituted for the freeze. All five contract `sourceFiles` were read through tools in full: interrogate SKILL.md, code-quality-review.md, lead-judgment.md, reviewer-prompt.md and rubric.md. The same frozen root supplied host-capabilities.md, model-roles.md and orchestration-contract.md. `source-reads.json` retains their full bytes, absolute paths and digests. No global skill, memory or history was consulted. Ancestor AGENTS.md checks found no files at the inspected ancestor paths; provided session instructions remained applicable.\n\nCommitted inputs were copied to `outputs/working/cache/` before execution. Only the separate `outputs/working/before/cache.mjs` copy was changed for the comparison. Pinned dependency SHA-256 was observed as `d33e8d36600ad5df008657e478e39f9100a347098b4176f37923e5056cb3427f`, matching the manifest. Conclusions concern this exact local vendored fixture; no public dependency behavior or host behavioral parity is claimed.\n\nArtifacts under this case's `outputs/`:\n\n- `supplied-input.json`, `source-reads.json`, `input-digests.json`, `reviewer-task.md`.\n- `assignment-a.json`, `assignment-b.json`: immutable assignments; final status recorded separately through events and inspected result metadata.\n- `native-spawn-receipts.json`: exact native spawn arguments and returns reconstructed from this run; this is not a second invocation.\n- `reviewer-a/result.md`, `reviewer-a/metadata.json`, `inspections/reviewer-a.json`; corresponding `reviewer-b/` artifacts.\n- `probe-results.json`: exact Bun process calls and their complete returns, including intentional exit 1.\n- `events.jsonl`, `capability-bindings.json`, `final-verification.json`: observed operations, limits and final artifact checks.\n- `working/cache/` and `working/before/`: actual execution inputs.\n\nRaw native tool calls also exist in the session transcript. Local JSON preserves exact spawn and probe receipts and full source reads; it does not claim to be an exhaustive raw transcript of every shell wrapper. No external services, installations, PRs, new commits, settings changes or automatic fixes occurred. Final repository verification checks committed input hashes and HEAD, and records any mismatch explicitly.\n\nThe declared case ceiling was 12 minutes. The exact task-start timestamp was not captured; first verified clock observation was 18:18:59 UTC on 2026-10-16. A conservative deadline used the earlier batch start, 18:16:58 UTC, yielding 18:28:58 UTC. Final observed clock and integrity checks are recorded in `final-verification.json`.\n\n\nFinal root readback confirms HEAD b437182f726bdb7cf322a783c5fbd947d9ab3214, committed-input diff exit 0, and status \"?? outputs/\". Exact arguments/returns are in raw/root-input-integrity.json. Active root event snapshot cutoff 2026-10-16T18:28:21.309Z is saved at raw/active-session-events.jsonl. Serializer gaps and incomplete nested worker captures remain disclosed. No self-grade is assigned.\n"

Challenge correctness and structural quality, then decide which findings warrant action.\",\n      \"requirements\": [\n        \"State intent and give every competing reviewer the identical diff, context, rubric and code-quality lens.\",\n        \"Preserve all rubric lenses, evidence-based findings and pragmatic lead judgment across act on, consider, noted and dismissed categories.\",\n        \"Report reviewer models, finding counts, independent consensus, lone findings and disagreements in the complete verdict format.\",\n        \"Verify independent worker results and observed model diversity; disclose sequential review as reduced capability.\",\n        \"Do not auto-apply changes or create external changes to fix rejected model defaults.\"\n      ],\n      \"expectedFull\": \"Authorized independent reviewers receive the same template and all rubric/code-quality lenses. The lead deduplicates findings, traces evidence and classifies every finding with attribution and rationale. The verdict includes Intent, Reviewers, Act On, Consider, Noted, Dismissed and Agreement Map, with observed model diversity or its explicit limit.\",\n      \"expectedReduced\": \"When independent workers are unavailable, an authorized sequential review applies the complete rubric and lead judgment and labels reduced capability. It does not claim independent agreement or model diversity; unknown actual models remain unknown.\",\n      \"expectedBlocked\": \"Unavailable diff or context, or denied review capability, blocks grounded findings. Report the missing source or permission without inventing findings, reviewer execution or agreement.\",\n      \"forbiddenClaims\": [\n        \"Sequential passes are independent reviewers.\",\n        \"Requested model names prove actual model diversity.\",\n        \"The rubric or code-quality lens can be omitted.\",\n        \"A review authorizes automatic fixes or an external PR.\",\n        \"Structural checks establish host behavioral parity.\"\n      ]\n    },\n    \"sourceFiles\": [\n      \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/SKILL.md\",\n      \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/code-quality-review.md\",\n      \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/lead-judgment.md\",\n      \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/reviewer-prompt.md\",\n      \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/rubric.md\"\n    ]\n  },\n  \"fixtures\": [],\n  \"supplementalTask\": \"Intent: change deferred invalidation to immediate deletion while preserving disposal correctness. Inspect all worker findings and lead-classify every deduplicated item into Act On/Consider/Noted/Dismissed, with attributed Agreement Map. Do not apply a fix or open a PR. Actual model diversity must be observed or limited explicitly.\",\n  \"chronologyGate\": null,\n  \"outputRoot\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-workflow-013-20261016/fixtures/cases/adversarial-review/outputs\"\n}"
