    {
      "path": "fixtures/cases/adversarial-review/outputs/source-reads.json",
      "bytes": 41277,
      "sha256": "268b34adad2174ea3051fa9b2909f4776494c9ecffba44e0a0b4a8fcb59b7076",
      "owner": "local-013-initial-adversarial-review",
      "kind": "case-owned-reviewed-artifact",
      "text": "[\n  {\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/SKILL.md\",\n    \"content\": \"---\\nname: interrogate\\ndescription: \\\"Use for \\\\\\\"interrogate\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\"adversarial review\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\"multi-model review\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\"challenge this\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\"stress test this code\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\"find blind spots\\\\\\\", or \\\\\\\"tear this apart\\\\\\\". Multiple LLM reviewers challenge changes from independent angles.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Interrogate\\n\\nSpawn one reviewer per configured model to adversarially review code changes. Each model gets the same prompt and rubric. The adversarial signal comes from model diversity, not assigned personas.\\n\\nThe deliverable is a synthesized verdict. Do NOT auto-apply changes.\\n\\n## Step 1, Determine Scope\\n\\nIdentify what to review from context:\\n\\n- If the user points at specific files or a diff, use that\\n- If on a feature branch, run `git diff main...HEAD` (or the appropriate base branch) for the full changeset\\n- If the user's message references recent work, gather the relevant files\\n\\nPackage the diff (or file contents) plus any surrounding context files the reviewers need to understand the code.\\n\\n## Step 2, State the Intent\\n\\nBefore spawning reviewers, state the intent explicitly. Derive this from:\\n\\n- The user's message\\n- Commit messages\\n- PR description if one exists\\n- The code itself\\n\\nWrite one clear paragraph. If you're unsure about the intent, ask the user before proceeding.\\n\\n## Step 3, Spawn Reviewers\\n\\nUse [session capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md), [model selection](../../references/model-roles.md), and the [assignment contract](../../references/orchestration-contract.md). Launch authorized independent read-only reviewers together through the exposed worker operation, one per explicitly configured reviewer selection. Extend or shrink Reviewer A/B labels to the selection count. Give each an immutable assignment, the same input revision and separate result ownership. Inspect actual results before synthesis.\\n\\nThe canonical source used a local model rule and default requests from the Claude and Grok families. The exact historical defaults are preserved as provenance in the [adaptation record](../../docs/parity/adaptations/interrogate.json). These historical requests do not establish available models. Preserve explicit session selections. With no configured selections, request two inherited reviewers and disclose that inherited or identical models do not establish model diversity. For auto or inherit-parent, omit explicit model selection. Never treat aliases as rejected model names.\\n\\nSelect explicit models and effort only from current exposed tool support. If a configured request is rejected, disclose it and use an authorized supported alternative, preferring the same family and reasoning tier where available. If the exact selection is required, report blocked. Record requested and observed models separately; missing observations remain unknown. Do not open a PR or change settings to repair upstream defaults without explicit authorization.\\n\\nWhen independent workers are unavailable, apply the same prompt and rubric sequentially with authorization and label reduced capability. Do not call repeated passes independent reviewers or consensus. Missing observed model diversity limits that claim even when worker independence succeeds. Denied review or missing source blocks grounded findings. Do not install tools or configure agents as an incidental step.\\n\\nRead `references/reviewer-prompt.md` and fill in the template with:\\n1. The stated intent\\n2. The diff or file contents\\n3. The review rubric from `references/rubric.md`\\n4. The code-quality lens from `references/code-quality-review.md`\\n\\nThe same filled template goes to all reviewers, so every model applies the code-quality lens.\\n\\n## Step 4, Synthesize\\n\\nAs results come back, build a unified picture:\\n\\n1. **Parse all findings** from the reviewers\\n2. **Identify consensus**. Findings raised by 2+ models independently are highest signal.\\n3. **Identify lone-model findings**. Still worth reading, but weight accordingly.\\n4. **Deduplicate**. Different models may describe the same issue differently. Merge these and note which models raised it.\\n5. **Note disagreements**. If one model flags something and another explicitly says the opposite, that's useful context for the verdict.\\n\\n## Step 5, Lead Judgment\\n\\nYou are the lead reviewer, a pragmatic senior engineer, not a neutral aggregator.\\n\\nRead `references/lead-judgment.md` for the full framework.\\n\\nCategorize every finding using these buckets:\\n\\n- **Act on**. Real issues affecting correctness, security, or maintainability given the actual goals. These would block a real PR.\\n- **Consider**. Legitimate points, but you're not sure they outweigh the cost of addressing them right now. Worth the user's attention.\\n- **Noted**. Technically valid but not actionable. Context-dependent, premature optimization, or low-impact given the current stage.\\n- **Dismissed**. Wrong, nitpicky, or missing context. Brief explanation why.\\n\\nFor each finding, include:\\n- Which model(s) raised it\\n- The category (act on / consider / noted / dismissed)\\n- A one-line rationale for the categorization\\n\\n## Output Format\\n\\nPresent the verdict in this structure:\\n\\n### Intent\\n> [The stated intent paragraph from Step 2]\\n\\n### Reviewers\\nReport requested and observed models, worker independence, model diversity and capability limits. Missing observations remain unknown.\\n\\n- Reviewer [label]: [model name], [N findings] (one bullet per reviewer)\\n\\n### Act On\\n[Findings that should be addressed. For each: description, which models raised it, why it matters.]\\n\\n### Consider\\n[Findings worth thinking about. For each: description, which models raised it, tradeoff involved.]\\n\\n### Noted\\n[Valid but low-priority. Brief list.]\\n\\n### Dismissed\\n[Rejected findings with brief rationale.]\\n\\n### Agreement Map\\n[Where did models agree, where did they diverge, and what does the pattern of agreement/disagreement tell us?]\\n\",\n    \"sha256\": \"7a6714b0bfea7c2eb4a533f95873c80e17b7b6f9ef6314c0c42e8947602ad11b\"\n  },\n  {\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/code-quality-review.md\",\n    \"content\": \"# Code Quality Review\\n\\nEach reviewer applies this code-quality lens in addition to the rubric. It is a strict standard focused on implementation quality, maintainability, abstraction quality, and codebase health.\\n\\nAbove all, be ambitious about code structure. Do not merely identify local cleanup. Actively search for \\\"code judo\\\" moves, restructurings that preserve behavior while making the implementation dramatically simpler, smaller, more direct, and more elegant.\\n\\n## Core Prompt\\n\\nStart from this baseline:\\n\\n> Perform a deep code quality audit of the current branch's changes.\\n> Rethink how to structure / implement the changes to meaningfully improve code quality without impacting behavior.\\n> Work to improve abstractions, modularity, reduce Spaghetti code, improve succinctness and legibility.\\n> Be ambitious, if there is a clear path to improving the implementation that involves restructuring some of the codebase, go for it.\\n> Be extremely thorough and rigorous. Measure twice, cut once.\\n\\n## Dimensions\\n\\nEach dimension is stated once. Apply the ones that are relevant.\\n\\n0. **Be ambitious about structural simplification.** Do not stop at \\\"this could be a bit cleaner.\\\" Look for reframings that make whole branches, helpers, modes, conditionals, or layers disappear. Assume a \\\"code judo\\\" move is often available. It uses the existing architecture more effectively and makes the change dramatically simpler. If you can delete complexity rather than rearrange it, push hard for that.\\n\\n1. **Do not let a PR push a file from under 1k lines to over 1k lines without a very strong reason.** Treat this as a strong smell. Prefer extracting helpers, subcomponents, or modules. If the diff crosses that threshold, ask whether the code should be decomposed first. Waive only for a compelling structural reason where the resulting file stays clearly organized.\\n\\n2. **Do not allow spaghetti growth in existing code.** Be suspicious of new ad-hoc conditionals, scattered special cases, or one-off branches inserted into unrelated flows. Treat \\\"weird if statements in random places\\\" as a design problem, not a style nit. Prefer pushing the logic into a dedicated helper, state machine, or module instead of tangling an existing path.\\n\\n3. **Bias toward cleaning the design, not just accepting working code.** If behavior can stay the same while the structure becomes meaningfully cleaner, push for the cleaner version. Prefer simplifications that remove moving pieces over refactors that spread the same complexity around.\\n\\n4. **Prefer direct, boring, maintainable code over hacky or magical code.** Treat brittle, ad-hoc, or \\\"magic\\\" behavior as a problem. Be skeptical of generic mechanisms that hide simple data-shape assumptions. Flag thin abstractions, identity wrappers, or pass-through helpers that add indirection without buying clarity.\\n\\n5. **Push on type and boundary cleanliness when it affects maintainability.** Question unnecessary optionality, `unknown`, `any`, or cast-heavy code when a clearer type boundary could exist. Prefer explicit typed models over loosely-shaped ad-hoc objects. If a branch leans on a silent fallback to paper over an unclear invariant, ask whether the boundary should be made explicit.\\n\\n6. **Keep logic in the canonical layer and reuse existing helpers.** Call out feature logic leaking into shared paths or implementation details leaking through APIs. Prefer existing canonical utilities over bespoke one-offs. Push code toward the right package, service, or module instead of normalizing drift.\\n\\n7. **Treat unnecessary sequential orchestration and non-atomic updates as design smells when the cleaner structure is obvious.** If independent work is serialized for no reason, ask whether it should run in parallel. If related updates can leave state half-applied, push for a more atomic structure. Do not over-index on micro-optimizations, but do flag avoidable orchestration complexity that makes the code more brittle.\\n\\n## Output Expectations\\n\\nPrioritize structural code-quality regressions and missed simplifications first, then spaghetti and branching complexity, then boundary, type, and file-size concerns, then smaller modularity and legibility issues.\\n\\n## Approval Bar\\n\\nDo not approve merely because behavior seems correct. Treat these as presumptive blockers unless the author can justify them: the PR keeps a lot of incidental complexity when a code-judo move would delete it. Pushes a file from below 1000 lines to above 1000 lines. Adds ad-hoc branching that tangles an existing flow. Scatters feature checks across shared code. Adds an unnecessary abstraction, wrapper, or cast-heavy contract, or duplicates an existing helper or puts logic in the wrong layer when there is a clear canonical home. If those conditions are not met, leave explicit, actionable feedback and push for a cleaner decomposition.\\n\\n## Review Tone\\n\\nBe direct, serious, and demanding about quality. Do not be rude, but do not soften major maintainability issues into mild suggestions. If the code is making the codebase messier, say so. If the implementation missed an obvious dramatic simplification, say that too. Do not be satisfied with \\\"maybe rename this\\\" when the real issue is structural.\\n\",\n    \"sha256\": \"79162fda183eafd40d51661fbb5e151c7ba250f89e506ba17f5bd135dcbd50a8\"\n  },\n  {\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/lead-judgment.md\",\n    \"content\": \"# Lead Judgment Framework\\n\\nYou are the lead reviewer. The configured reviewers have produced their findings. Apply pragmatic engineering judgment. Don't aggregate. Filter, contextualize, and decide.\\n\\n## Why This Step Matters\\n\\nAdversarial reviewers are useful because they're aggressive. But aggression without context produces noise. The reviewers only saw a slice of the codebase and a one-paragraph intent statement. They don't know:\\n\\n- What was already tried and rejected\\n- What constraints exist outside the code (timeline, dependencies, migration plans)\\n- Which parts of the code are temporary scaffolding vs. permanent architecture\\n- What the next PR in the stack will address\\n\\nYou have the full conversation context. Use it.\\n\\n## Filtering Principles\\n\\n### Nitpick Gravity\\n\\nReviewers, especially adversarial ones, tend to fill their review. If they don't find critical issues, they'll inflate nits to fill the space. If a reviewer's findings are all nits and style preferences, the code is probably fine. Say so.\\n\\n### Hypothetical vs. Actual\\n\\n\\\"What if someone passes null here?\\\" is only a finding if the caller can actually pass null. Trace the call site. If the input is validated upstream or the type system prevents it, dismiss the finding. Reviewers working from a diff can't always see the full call chain. You can.\\n\\n### Premature Abstraction Warnings\\n\\nReviewers often suggest extracting functions, adding interfaces, or creating abstractions. Does this code need to change in a second way? If not, the abstraction is premature. Simple inline code that works beats a clean abstraction that's overkill for the current scope.\\n\\n### \\\"I Would Have Done It Differently\\\"\\n\\nThis is the most common false positive in code review. A finding that amounts to \\\"I prefer a different approach\\\" is not a bug, not a design flaw, and not actionable unless the reviewer shows a concrete problem with the current approach. Dismiss these, and say why.\\n\\n### Missing Context Signals\\n\\nWatch for findings that reveal the reviewer didn't understand the context:\\n- Suggesting changes to code the author didn't write or modify\\n- Flagging patterns that are consistent with the rest of the codebase (the reviewer just doesn't know that)\\n- Recommending approaches that conflict with constraints you know about\\n\\nThese are honest mistakes from reviewers working with limited information. Dismiss them gracefully.\\n\\n## When Reviewers Are Right\\n\\nDon't dismiss findings just because they're uncomfortable. The whole point of adversarial review is to catch things you'd miss. Signs a finding deserves attention:\\n\\n- Multiple models flag the same issue independently (consensus signal)\\n- The finding identifies a concrete execution path, not a hypothetical\\n- The finding reveals a gap in your mental model of the code\\n- You read the finding and think \\\"...yeah, actually\\\"\\n\\nBe especially careful about dismissing security findings and correctness bugs. These deserve more scrutiny even when they come from a single model.\\n\\n## Verdict Calibration\\n\\nA good verdict is useful, not comprehensive. The user should be able to read the \\\"Act On\\\" section, fix those issues, and ship with confidence. If your \\\"Act On\\\" list has more than 5 items, you're probably not filtering hard enough.\\n\\nThe \\\"Dismissed\\\" section is not busywork. It's a trust mechanism. Showing the user what you rejected and why lets them override your judgment where they disagree. This is more valuable than hiding the rejected findings.\\n\",\n    \"sha256\": \"6e5bdf5670eb34017692e9b6c546f36ac7552e6bb9f3f2f3429164fcc46a9361\"\n  },\n  {\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/reviewer-prompt.md\",\n    \"content\": \"# Reviewer Prompt Template\\n\\nBuild each reviewer subagent's prompt from this template, filling in the placeholders.\\n\\n---\\n\\nYou are an adversarial code reviewer. Find real problems in the code below: bugs, design flaws, security issues, and maintainability concerns. You are not here to be helpful or encouraging. You are here to stress-test.\\n\\n## Intent\\n\\nThe author's stated intent for this change:\\n\\n> {INTENT}\\n\\nYou are reviewing whether the code achieves this intent well. Do NOT question the intent itself. Assume the goal is correct and challenge the execution.\\n\\n## Code Under Review\\n\\n{DIFF_OR_FILES}\\n\\n## Review Rubric\\n\\n{RUBRIC_CONTENTS}\\n\\n## Code Quality Lens\\n\\n{CODE_QUALITY_CONTENTS}\\n\\n## Instructions\\n\\nReview the code through every lens in the rubric and the code-quality lens above that you find relevant. Do not force lenses that don't apply. A simple bug fix does not need paragraphs about architectural integrity.\\n\\nFor each finding, provide:\\n\\n1. **Severity**: `critical` | `warning` | `nit`\\n   - `critical`: Would cause bugs, data loss, security issues, or fundamentally broken behavior\\n   - `warning`: Design concern, maintainability risk, or correctness issue that isn't immediately broken but will cause pain\\n   - `nit`: Style, naming, minor improvement.\\n2. **Finding**: What the problem is, in concrete terms. Reference specific lines/functions.\\n3. **Evidence**: Why you believe this is a problem. Show your reasoning. Don't just assert.\\n4. **Suggestion** (optional): What you'd do instead, if you have a concrete alternative. Skip this if you don't have a clear fix.\\n\\n## What Makes a Good Finding\\n\\n- It references specific code, not vague concerns (\\\"this could be better\\\")\\n- It explains WHY something is a problem, not just THAT it is\\n- It distinguishes between \\\"this is broken\\\" and \\\"I would have done this differently\\\"\\n- It considers the stated intent. A finding that ignores the context of what's being built is a bad finding\\n\\n## What to Avoid\\n\\n- Restating what the code does without identifying a problem\\n- Praising the code. You're an adversary, not a cheerleader. If you find nothing wrong, say \\\"no findings\\\" and stop.\\n\\n## Output\\n\\nReturn your findings as a structured list. If you have zero findings, say so. An empty review is a valid outcome.\\n\\n```\\n## Findings\\n\\n### 1. [Severity] Short title\\n**Location**: file:line or function name\\n**Finding**: What's wrong\\n**Evidence**: Why this matters\\n**Suggestion**: (optional) What to do instead\\n\\n### 2. [Severity] Short title\\n...\\n```\\n\",\n    \"sha256\": \"e0598e254792b56de39bb16b1e6db559b03a522fdc54b98f942d254090920ae6\"\n  },\n  {\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/rubric.md\",\n    \"content\": \"# Review Rubric\\n\\nReview through whichever lenses are relevant. Not every lens applies to every change. Use judgment.\\n\\n## Correctness\\n\\nDoes the code actually do what the intent says it should?\\n\\n- Edge cases: empty inputs, nil/undefined, boundary values, concurrent access\\n- Error handling: are errors caught, propagated, or silently swallowed?\\n- Off-by-one, type coercion, integer overflow, string encoding\\n- State management: race conditions, stale closures, dangling references\\n- Does the happy path work? Does the sad path work?\\n- Idempotency: what happens if this operation runs twice, or if a previous run crashed halfway? If the answer is \\\"it depends on what state was left behind,\\\" there's a missing reconciliation step.\\n- Concurrency: if multiple actors can touch the same mutable state (files, branches, shared data), is access serialized structurally (locks, sequential phases, exclusive ownership), or by conventions that won't hold?\\n\\nWhen you find a potential bug, trace the execution path. Don't just flag \\\"this could be nil\\\". Show the call chain that makes it nil.\\n\\n## Root Causes vs. Symptoms\\n\\nIs the code fixing the actual problem or papering over a symptom?\\n\\nAnswering this often requires looking beyond the changed files. Read the surrounding code (callers, callees, type definitions, sibling modules) and understand the architecture the change lives in. Use the tools available to you (Read, Grep, Glob) to explore. Follow the call chain. Read the types. Understand why the code exists before judging whether the change addresses the right layer.\\n\\n- Guard clauses that mask a deeper invariant violation\\n- Retry logic that hides a broken contract\\n- Type casts that silence a modeling error\\n- If you see a workaround, ask: why is the workaround needed? What would a proper fix look like?\\n- A fix in module A that should really be a fix in module B's contract\\n- Instructions where structure would be better: if the fix is a comment saying \\\"don't do X\\\" or a convention someone has to remember, ask whether it could instead be a type constraint, a lint rule, or a runtime check that makes the wrong thing impossible\\n\\n## Structural Integrity\\n\\nDoes the code fit well into the system it's part of?\\n\\n- Boundary discipline: is validation at system boundaries, or scattered through business logic? Validate data once where it enters the system, then trust it internally.\\n- Abstraction level: is the code mixing high-level orchestration with low-level detail?\\n- Coupling: does this change introduce dependencies that will make future changes harder?\\n- Data model fit: do the data structures match the actual access patterns? The right structure makes downstream code obvious. The wrong one fights you at every turn.\\n- Bolted-on vs. integrated: was the change patched onto the existing design, or does it read as if the design always accounted for it? If the new requirement had been known from the start, would the code look like this?\\n- Legacy dual-paths: does the change introduce a new API while keeping the old one alive? If there are no external consumers, migrate callers and delete the old path in the same wave. Don't leave compatibility layers that will become permanent.\\n\\nDon't penalize simple code for lacking abstraction. Premature abstraction is worse than duplication.\\n\\n## Verification\\n\\nCan you tell that this code works from reading it?\\n\\n- Are there tests? Do they test behavior or implementation details?\\n- Are there assertions/invariants that would catch regressions?\\n- If this is a bug fix: is there a test for the bug?\\n- If this touches an integration boundary: is the full path tested?\\n- Check the real thing, not a proxy. If the code checks liveness via file mtime or cached state instead of reading the actual value, that's a verification gap.\\n- For delegated or async work: does the code verify actual output artifacts, or does it trust self-reports and summaries?\\n\\n## Complexity Budget\\n\\nIs the complexity justified by what the code accomplishes?\\n\\n- Code that could be simpler without losing correctness or clarity\\n- Abstractions that serve only one call site\\n- Configuration or parameterization for cases that don't exist yet\\n- Dead code, unused imports, vestigial parameters\\n- Over-engineering: \\\"just in case\\\" code paths with no current callers\\n- Obsolete compatibility paths kept alive for transitional stability that's no longer needed. If the migration is done, delete the scaffolding\\n- Does the user experience justify the complexity? Every feature, control, and option should earn its place. Half-finished features are worse than missing ones.\\n\\nSimpler is better unless simpler is wrong. Three lines of duplication beat a premature abstraction.\\n\\n## Security\\n\\nFor each security finding, trace the input path through the code and show it.\\n\\n- User input flowing to dangerous sinks (SQL, shell, eval, innerHTML) without sanitization\\n- Authentication/authorization gaps in new endpoints\\n- Secrets in code, logs, or error messages\\n- TOCTOU (time-of-check-time-of-use) in security-critical paths\\n\",\n    \"sha256\": \"a85aa801abbaf14434f00579440abc0d8e2723227ba8bf57de7cca8d6e7d2f25\"\n  },\n  {\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/references/host-capabilities.md\",\n    \"content\": \"# Discover capabilities in the current session\\n\\nRead the tools exposed to this session before assigning work. Cached official-subagents.txt is documentation context; it proves no operation succeeded. Local agent configuration is not portable plugin configuration.\\n\\nRecord each binding as `{operation, tool, exposed, succeeded, evidence, limits}`. `tool` is the exact exposed identifier or null when absent. `succeeded` is true only after observing a result. Keep failed and unavailable bindings. Successful bindings become `successfulOperations` in host evidence, with a root-relative evidence file. The operation labels below describe purposes, never callable APIs.\\n\\n| Operation | Discovery and observation required |\\n| --- | --- |\\n| spawn | Find an exposed independent-worker operation; retain returned worker identity and exact assigned revision. |\\n| follow-up | Find the operation that sends input to that worker; observe acceptance by the same identity. |\\n| wait | Find the operation that waits for worker state; retain terminal or interrupted state. |\\n| inspect-result | Read the worker result and its referenced artifacts; a wait notification alone is insufficient. |\\n| stop | Observe cancellation or termination through the exposed operation. |\\n| read | Observe the requested file content through the available file tool. |\\n| isolated-write | Observe a write within the worker's assigned path and permissions. |\\n| history | Observe available prior turns or records; declare gaps explicitly. |\\n| execution | Observe command execution with exit status and output. |\\n| persistence | Write and read an authorized durable record; a claimed save is insufficient. |\\n| external-write | Record explicit authorization and action identity; inspect the result before replay. |\\n| resolve-ownership | Observe distinct owned paths before contested writes. |\\n| inspect-terminal | Observe the worker's terminal state after stop. |\\n| quarantine-artifacts | Observe partial artifacts withheld from integration. |\\n| inspect-prior-action | Read the interrupted action identity and prior outcome. |\\n| inspect-destination | Observe the destination state for that action identity. |\\n| safe-recovery | Observe explicitly authorized idempotent recovery after identity and destination inspection. |\\n\\nNo default API names are assigned here. Tool names differ by host and session. An exposed tool may still be denied or fail. Record requested and actual models only when observed; otherwise use `unknown`. A requested model never proves the actual model.\\n\\nA denied or unavailable operation blocks the branch that requires it. Sequential substitution for required independent work is reduced capability and uses `reduced-pass`; ordinary direct completion can fully pass. Missing persistence limits session pickup even when current work succeeds. Preserve user authorization and host permission boundaries through every fallback.\\n\\nWorker passes require observed spawn and result inspection. Each registry entry declares `requiredObservations` for `pass` and `reduced-pass`; tool observations need exposed bindings and existing evidence artifacts; native text observations use the separate reviewed native record below. Full cancellation requires stop, terminal inspection and artifact quarantine. Full persistence requires durable write and readback. Full interrupted-action recovery requires prior identity inspection, destination inspection, safe recovery, explicit external permission and a recorded prior action identity even when no external-write observation is listed.\\n\\nPermission checks follow performed operations. Isolated writes and persistence require granted writes. External writes and safe recovery require granted external permission and prior identity. Authorized read-only reductions may retain denied writes; their limits must preserve denied or unavailable permissions. Required observations remain applicable to those reductions.\\n\\nHost evidence is stored as object or array JSON files under `docs/parity/evidence/`. Required fields are defined by `docs/parity/evidence.schema.json`. Artifacts and operation evidence must be existing files inside the tested root. Test fixtures belong in `tests/` and prove validator behavior only.\\n\\nRun `python3 tools/validate_evidence.py --root . --commit <tested-package-commit>` through Bun, for example `bun -e 'const p=Bun.spawnSync([\\\"python3\\\",\\\"-B\\\",\\\"tools/validate_evidence.py\\\",\\\"--root\\\",\\\".\\\",\\\"--commit\\\",process.argv[1]],{env:{...process.env,PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE:\\\"1\\\"}});process.stdout.write(p.stdout);process.stderr.write(p.stderr);process.exit(p.exitCode)' <tested-package-commit>`. It reads every JSON record below the evidence directory, prints each error, and exits 1 for invalid records or 0 for valid records. An empty directory has no invalid records and supplies no behavioral evidence.\\n\\nThe required registry file in `docs/parity/case-registry.json` declares applicability. Missing or non-file registry, unknown case, structural-only entry or unlisted host rejects behavioral validation. Maintenance fixtures use a temporary copy of this registry; they cannot bypass applicability. Required hosts must receive observed evidence or explicit blocked records before release claims. Structural checks have no required host and cannot populate host status. Later producers propose task-owned entries; only the coordinator integrates them. Source paths select callers through the task 3 case contract. Case definitions alone leave host statuses `not-run`.\\n\\n## Direct completion and recorded observations\\n\\n`direct` means the task is completed in the current context. Restatement, supplied-text writing and complete installed discovery can pass directly. `sequential` is reserved for replacing a required independent method with sequential work and remains reduced. A text response has no invented callable tool. Record supplied input, the actual response and qualitative outcome review as `nativeObservations`, each with its existing listed evidence artifact and named reviewer. Their labels are `supplied-input`, `response` and `review-outcome`. The reviewer assesses the case's expected outcome; validation checks evidence relationships and does not decide prose quality.\\n\\nEach registry entry declares allowed execution modes and required permissions for each status. A grant is required for the performed action: worker spawn, command execution and stop need execution; isolated output or persistence needs writes; external actions need their separate permission and prior identity. Read-only discovery does not inherit an execution grant requirement. Tool observations still name an actually exposed tool and existing evidence. Native observations cannot claim tool discovery, worker identity or saved writes.\\n\\nFull Arena and Swarm results additionally provide `inputRevision` and a listed `methodReceipt` artifact. An exposed `inspect-method` operation must reference that receipt. Assignment operations name `assignmentId` and the corresponding inspected output. See the [structured method contract](orchestration-contract.md). All positive maintenance fixtures are synthetic; none is an observed host result.\\n\",\n    \"sha256\": \"f0f09ce28b6feca4d72694d5687204dc074aa58dade9a0ab59656da3bfa1bd20\"\n  },\n  {\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/references/model-roles.md\",\n    \"content\": \"# Model selection for portable roles\\n\\nUse [session capabilities](host-capabilities.md) before assigning a role. These [poteto](agent-prompts/poteto-agent.md) and [comment reviewer](agent-prompts/comment-sicko.md) prompts are instructions supplied to an exposed worker operation. Reading them does not register a custom agent or authorize delegation.\\n\\n| Role | Default requested model | Observed model | Supported effort | Fallback disclosure |\\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\\n| poteto-agent | inherit-parent | unknown until observed | inherit-parent unless a supported explicit effort is verified | Record rejected selection, replacement request, observed result and limits. |\\n| comment-sicko | inherit-parent | unknown until observed | inherit-parent unless a supported explicit effort is verified | Record rejected selection, replacement request, observed result and limits. |\\n\\nFor each assignment, record requested model and effort, observed model and effort, supported effort values and their source, worker identity, and fallback disclosure. Use `unknown` for information the operation does not expose. `inherit-parent` records a selection policy, not an observed model name. Do not silently convert a requested model into an actual model.\\n\\nSelect an explicit model only from current exposed tool documentation or observed enumeration results. If enumeration is missing, disclose that limit and retain `inherit-parent`. Documentation examples are not a list of available models. Use only an effort supported for the selected model by the current operation; do not carry an incompatible inherited effort into an explicit selection.\\n\\nIf a selected model or effort is rejected, retain the rejection. Use an authorized inherited or supported alternative only after disclosing the changed selection. If the task requires that exact selection, stop and record blocked. A fallback never proves the original selection succeeded.\\n\\nIdentical observed models mean no model diversity. Different roles can supply distinct instructions on the same model. Unknown observed models leave diversity unverified, even when requested model names differ. Different observed models support a model-diversity statement only for those observed assignments. Worker independence still requires successful spawn and result inspection.\\n\\nWhen workers are unavailable, apply an authorized role sequentially and label reduced capability. Preserve scope and permission boundaries. Do not claim independent review, background execution, or full worker support from sequential role switching.\\n\\nThe cached documentation from https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/agent-configuration/subagents describes inheritance and optional local agent configuration. It is context only. This package writes no local custom-agent configuration and claims no current host model or worker support from that documentation.\\n\",\n    \"sha256\": \"c86696b6c568b7db80a7e339856986e64482ce6a418bb91946ce3a3b772f79a1\"\n  },\n  {\n    \"path\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/references/orchestration-contract.md\",\n    \"content\": \"# Assignment and integration contract\\n\\nBefore dispatch, the coordinator creates an immutable assignment with `id`, `scope`, `inputRevision`, `outputOwner`, `isolatedOutputPath`, `role`, `retryCount`, `terminalStatus`, and `evidenceLinks`. Use the full Git SHA for inputRevision, an exact owned path, and an explicit role. Start retryCount at 0 and terminalStatus at `pending`. Preserve every attempt as an append-only event under the same assignment ID; update status through events, never rewrite the original scope, revision, owner or path. A changed scope or revision requires a new assignment ID that links its predecessor.\\n\\nThe coordinator checks authorization and observed host bindings before dispatch. One path has one writer. Reject overlapping paths before work starts; assign distinct paths instead. Workers write only their isolated output and report results. Only the coordinator integrates shared files, inventory, registry, or other workers' output.\\n\\nAccept a result only when its assignment identity and input SHA match, its artifacts exist, and required method evidence was observed. Reject stale revisions. Permit at most one retry for missing required SHA or method evidence, after inspecting the prior attempt and confirming the same permissions still apply. A retry count of 1 exhausts this allowance. Denied permissions, unknown action outcomes, and missing artifacts never become success by retry policy.\\n\\nTerminal states are `completed`, `blocked`, `failed`, and `cancelled`. A completion event links the exact result and evidence. Stop a cancelled worker through the exposed stop binding when available, then inspect its terminal state. Quarantine any partial artifacts; cancellation does not authorize their integration. If stop cannot be observed, record that limit and do not claim termination. Report interrupted execution explicitly.\\n\\nBefore an external action, record explicit permission, a stable action identity, the intended destination and the last observed outcome. After interruption, inspect whether that identity already completed. Retry only with authorization, an observed safe replay decision and an idempotency mechanism appropriate to the destination. If the outcome is unknown, block replay. Do not infer permission from upstream autonomy instructions.\\n\\nHistory and persistence have separate bindings. Keep assignment events and result links in an authorized durable location and verify readback. If unavailable, declare the gap and offer an authorized local handoff as reduced capability; never claim unattended recovery or cross-session continuity. All host statuses remain `not-run` until actual observations exist.\\n\\n## Structured Arena and Swarm receipts\\n\\nA method receipt is JSON with schemaVersion 1, kind `arena` or `swarm`, inputRevision, assignments, reviewArtifact, reviewedBy and declaredOrder. The inputRevision is the actual task repository revision, distinct from the packageCommit identifying these instructions. It must match the evidence record. The declared order precedes every dispatch. reviewArtifact records the qualitative outcome review and reviewedBy identifies its reviewer. All referenced artifacts must exist and be listed in the evidence record.\\n\\nEach assignment records id, workerId, inputRevision, outputOwner, outputPath, role, status, startedOrder, completedOrder, requestedModel, actualModel, retryCount, method, taskArtifact, rubricProvided, resultRevision, resultMethod and outputSha256. Result revision and method must match the assignment, and the inspected output bytes must match their digest. Worker and assignment identities are distinct. outputOwner equals workerId; output directories do not overlap. Integer order values record the observed dispatch and completion sequence, not estimated time. The method has positive sampleCount, sampleDefinition and order. Missing model observations use unknown. The validator requires assignment-bound spawn and result inspection, and isolated-write for output-producing candidates. A read-only judge may return its result through the coordinator's authorized artifact capture. A cancelled result needs bound stop, terminal inspection and quarantine observations. No operation string alone establishes a method.\\n\\nArena adds candidates, judge, rubricArtifact and synthesis. At least two candidates share the same taskArtifact, finish before a distinct fresh judge starts and do not receive the parent rubric. Synthesis has order, inspectedCandidates, artifact and verificationArtifact; it follows completed judgment and accounts for every candidate. The review artifact assesses scoring, base selection, grafts, rejections and verification quality. File presence and phase ordering do not establish the quality of those decisions.\\n\\nSwarm adds partition, race, cancelled and quarantined. Partition declares requiredSlices, results mapping each slice to its assignment, and gaps. A full coverage claim requires all slices with completed inspected outputs; gaps remain explicit reduced or blocked work. Race declares rule, assignments, winner and ruleArtifact. first-pass must select the first completed survivor; rank-all needs all results; best-of retains an explicit reviewed selection. Cancelled assignments must be quarantined and cannot enter the aggregate. The combined partition-and-race case requires both methods and its cancellation exercise. The partition walkthrough exercises only its declared slices; it does not acquire an unrelated race requirement.\\n\\nThe three-file Swarm walkthrough requires exactly three declared slices and three completed results. Its partition-only receipt uses race null and empty cancellation, quarantine and failureExercises arrays. The combined case also records failureExercises. Each event has assignmentId, kind, attempt, order, reason and evidence, with bound result-rejection or terminal inspection. Rejected missing-revision or missing-method evidence uses attempts 0 then 1; a second invalid result exhausts the retry and leaves a failed or blocked assignment. A separate failed or blocked dropout is disclosed. These failed outputs cannot satisfy required coverage. The final assignment result metadata records the inspected terminal summary; rejection-event artifacts retain the invalid attempts.\\n\",\n    \"sha256\": \"5dd740ad66d992df252754d189d0dc207900023372392a0db4309b85eb5e4ec5\"\n  }\n]"

      "text": "# Reviewer Prompt Template\n\nBuild each reviewer subagent's prompt from this template, filling in the placeholders.\n\n---\n\nYou are an adversarial code reviewer. Find real problems in the code below: bugs, design flaws, security issues, and maintainability concerns. You are not here to be helpful or encouraging. You are here to stress-test.\n\n## Intent\n\nThe author's stated intent for this change:\n\n> Change deferred invalidation to immediate deletion while preserving disposal correctness.\n\nYou are reviewing whether the code achieves this intent well. Do NOT question the intent itself. Assume the goal is correct and challenge the execution.\n\n## Code Under Review\n\nInput revision: b437182f726bdb7cf322a783c5fbd947d9ab3214\n\n### README.md\n```\nReview invalidation.diff against the after-state cache.mjs and the real caller outside the diff. The vendored disposer is the exact locally pinned dependency, not a mock pretending to prove a public dependency. Determine whether synchronous invalidation races the deferred disposal callback. probe.mjs runs this actual supplied implementation and intentionally exits nonzero if teardown throws. A before-state copy may restore the removed setTimeout line to compare ordering. No execution result is supplied.\n```\n\n### manifest.json\n```\n{\n  \"scope\": \"exact local vendored fixture only\",\n  \"dependency\": {\n    \"path\": \"vendor/disposer-v1.mjs\",\n    \"version\": \"fixture-v1\",\n    \"sha256\": \"d33e8d36600ad5df008657e478e39f9100a347098b4176f37923e5056cb3427f\"\n  },\n  \"publicPackageClaim\": false\n}\n```\n\n### invalidation.diff\n```\ndiff --git a/cache.mjs b/cache.mjs\n--- a/cache.mjs\n+++ b/cache.mjs\n@@ -1,4 +1,4 @@\n const entries = new Map();\n export function put(key, value) { entries.set(key, value); }\n export function get(key) { return entries.get(key); }\n-export function invalidate(key) { setTimeout(() => entries.delete(key), 0); }\n+export function invalidate(key) { entries.delete(key); }\n```\n\n### cache.mjs\n```\nconst entries = new Map();\nexport function put(key, value) { entries.set(key, value); }\nexport function get(key) { return entries.get(key); }\nexport function invalidate(key) { entries.delete(key); }\n```\n\n### caller.mjs\n```\nimport { createDisposer } from './vendor/disposer-v1.mjs';\nimport { get, put, invalidate } from './cache.mjs';\nexport function mount(key, report) {\n  put(key, { ready: true });\n  const subscription = createDisposer(() => report(get(key).ready));\n  return () => { subscription.dispose(); invalidate(key); };\n}\n```\n\n### vendor/disposer-v1.mjs\n```\n// Exact vendored fixture dependency implementation, pinned by local SHA-256 in manifest.json.\nexport function createDisposer(onDispose) {\n  return { dispose: () => queueMicrotask(onDispose) };\n}\n```\n\n### probe.mjs\n```\nimport { mount } from './caller.mjs';\nlet error;\nprocess.once('uncaughtException', caught => { error = caught; });\nconst unmount = mount('fixture-key', ready => console.log('disposed ready:', ready));\nunmount();\nawait new Promise(resolve => setTimeout(resolve, 5));\nconsole.log(error ? `teardown error: ${error.name}: ${error.message}` : 'teardown completed');\nprocess.exitCode = error ? 1 : 0;\n```\n\n\n## Review Rubric\n\n# Review Rubric\n\nReview through whichever lenses are relevant. Not every lens applies to every change. Use judgment.\n\n## Correctness\n\nDoes the code actually do what the intent says it should?\n\n- Edge cases: empty inputs, nil/undefined, boundary values, concurrent access\n- Error handling: are errors caught, propagated, or silently swallowed?\n- Off-by-one, type coercion, integer overflow, string encoding\n- State management: race conditions, stale closures, dangling references\n- Does the happy path work? Does the sad path work?\n- Idempotency: what happens if this operation runs twice, or if a previous run crashed halfway? If the answer is \"it depends on what state was left behind,\" there's a missing reconciliation step.\n- Concurrency: if multiple actors can touch the same mutable state (files, branches, shared data), is access serialized structurally (locks, sequential phases, exclusive ownership), or by conventions that won't hold?\n\nWhen you find a potential bug, trace the execution path. Don't just flag \"this could be nil\". Show the call chain that makes it nil.\n\n## Root Causes vs. Symptoms\n\nIs the code fixing the actual problem or papering over a symptom?\n\nAnswering this often requires looking beyond the changed files. Read the surrounding code (callers, callees, type definitions, sibling modules) and understand the architecture the change lives in. Use the tools available to you (Read, Grep, Glob) to explore. Follow the call chain. Read the types. Understand why the code exists before judging whether the change addresses the right layer.\n\n- Guard clauses that mask a deeper invariant violation\n- Retry logic that hides a broken contract\n- Type casts that silence a modeling error\n- If you see a workaround, ask: why is the workaround needed? What would a proper fix look like?\n- A fix in module A that should really be a fix in module B's contract\n- Instructions where structure would be better: if the fix is a comment saying \"don't do X\" or a convention someone has to remember, ask whether it could instead be a type constraint, a lint rule, or a runtime check that makes the wrong thing impossible\n\n## Structural Integrity\n\nDoes the code fit well into the system it's part of?\n\n- Boundary discipline: is validation at system boundaries, or scattered through business logic? Validate data once where it enters the system, then trust it internally.\n- Abstraction level: is the code mixing high-level orchestration with low-level detail?\n- Coupling: does this change introduce dependencies that will make future changes harder?\n- Data model fit: do the data structures match the actual access patterns? The right structure makes downstream code obvious. The wrong one fights you at every turn.\n- Bolted-on vs. integrated: was the change patched onto the existing design, or does it read as if the design always accounted for it? If the new requirement had been known from the start, would the code look like this?\n- Legacy dual-paths: does the change introduce a new API while keeping the old one alive? If there are no external consumers, migrate callers and delete the old path in the same wave. Don't leave compatibility layers that will become permanent.\n\nDon't penalize simple code for lacking abstraction. Premature abstraction is worse than duplication.\n\n## Verification\n\nCan you tell that this code works from reading it?\n\n- Are there tests? Do they test behavior or implementation details?\n- Are there assertions/invariants that would catch regressions?\n- If this is a bug fix: is there a test for the bug?\n- If this touches an integration boundary: is the full path tested?\n- Check the real thing, not a proxy. If the code checks liveness via file mtime or cached state instead of reading the actual value, that's a verification gap.\n- For delegated or async work: does the code verify actual output artifacts, or does it trust self-reports and summaries?\n\n## Complexity Budget\n\nIs the complexity justified by what the code accomplishes?\n\n- Code that could be simpler without losing correctness or clarity\n- Abstractions that serve only one call site\n- Configuration or parameterization for cases that don't exist yet\n- Dead code, unused imports, vestigial parameters\n- Over-engineering: \"just in case\" code paths with no current callers\n- Obsolete compatibility paths kept alive for transitional stability that's no longer needed. If the migration is done, delete the scaffolding\n- Does the user experience justify the complexity? Every feature, control, and option should earn its place. Half-finished features are worse than missing ones.\n\nSimpler is better unless simpler is wrong. Three lines of duplication beat a premature abstraction.\n\n## Security\n\nFor each security finding, trace the input path through the code and show it.\n\n- User input flowing to dangerous sinks (SQL, shell, eval, innerHTML) without sanitization\n- Authentication/authorization gaps in new endpoints\n- Secrets in code, logs, or error messages\n- TOCTOU (time-of-check-time-of-use) in security-critical paths\n\n\n## Code Quality Lens\n\n# Code Quality Review\n\nEach reviewer applies this code-quality lens in addition to the rubric. It is a strict standard focused on implementation quality, maintainability, abstraction quality, and codebase health.\n\nAbove all, be ambitious about code structure. Do not merely identify local cleanup. Actively search for \"code judo\" moves, restructurings that preserve behavior while making the implementation dramatically simpler, smaller, more direct, and more elegant.\n\n## Core Prompt\n\nStart from this baseline:\n\n> Perform a deep code quality audit of the current branch's changes.\n> Rethink how to structure / implement the changes to meaningfully improve code quality without impacting behavior.\n> Work to improve abstractions, modularity, reduce Spaghetti code, improve succinctness and legibility.\n> Be ambitious, if there is a clear path to improving the implementation that involves restructuring some of the codebase, go for it.\n> Be extremely thorough and rigorous. Measure twice, cut once.\n\n## Dimensions\n\nEach dimension is stated once. Apply the ones that are relevant.\n\n0. **Be ambitious about structural simplification.** Do not stop at \"this could be a bit cleaner.\" Look for reframings that make whole branches, helpers, modes, conditionals, or layers disappear. Assume a \"code judo\" move is often available. It uses the existing architecture more effectively and makes the change dramatically simpler. If you can delete complexity rather than rearrange it, push hard for that.\n\n1. **Do not let a PR push a file from under 1k lines to over 1k lines without a very strong reason.** Treat this as a strong smell. Prefer extracting helpers, subcomponents, or modules. If the diff crosses that threshold, ask whether the code should be decomposed first. Waive only for a compelling structural reason where the resulting file stays clearly organized.\n\n2. **Do not allow spaghetti growth in existing code.** Be suspicious of new ad-hoc conditionals, scattered special cases, or one-off branches inserted into unrelated flows. Treat \"weird if statements in random places\" as a design problem, not a style nit. Prefer pushing the logic into a dedicated helper, state machine, or module instead of tangling an existing path.\n\n3. **Bias toward cleaning the design, not just accepting working code.** If behavior can stay the same while the structure becomes meaningfully cleaner, push for the cleaner version. Prefer simplifications that remove moving pieces over refactors that spread the same complexity around.\n\n4. **Prefer direct, boring, maintainable code over hacky or magical code.** Treat brittle, ad-hoc, or \"magic\" behavior as a problem. Be skeptical of generic mechanisms that hide simple data-shape assumptions. Flag thin abstractions, identity wrappers, or pass-through helpers that add indirection without buying clarity.\n\n5. **Push on type and boundary cleanliness when it affects maintainability.** Question unnecessary optionality, `unknown`, `any`, or cast-heavy code when a clearer type boundary could exist. Prefer explicit typed models over loosely-shaped ad-hoc objects. If a branch leans on a silent fallback to paper over an unclear invariant, ask whether the boundary should be made explicit.\n\n6. **Keep logic in the canonical layer and reuse existing helpers.** Call out feature logic leaking into shared paths or implementation details leaking through APIs. Prefer existing canonical utilities over bespoke one-offs. Push code toward the right package, service, or module instead of normalizing drift.\n\n7. **Treat unnecessary sequential orchestration and non-atomic updates as design smells when the cleaner structure is obvious.** If independent work is serialized for no reason, ask whether it should run in parallel. If related updates can leave state half-applied, push for a more atomic structure. Do not over-index on micro-optimizations, but do flag avoidable orchestration complexity that makes the code more brittle.\n\n## Output Expectations\n\nPrioritize structural code-quality regressions and missed simplifications first, then spaghetti and branching complexity, then boundary, type, and file-size concerns, then smaller modularity and legibility issues.\n\n## Approval Bar\n\nDo not approve merely because behavior seems correct. Treat these as presumptive blockers unless the author can justify them: the PR keeps a lot of incidental complexity when a code-judo move would delete it. Pushes a file from below 1000 lines to above 1000 lines. Adds ad-hoc branching that tangles an existing flow. Scatters feature checks across shared code. Adds an unnecessary abstraction, wrapper, or cast-heavy contract, or duplicates an existing helper or puts logic in the wrong layer when there is a clear canonical home. If those conditions are not met, leave explicit, actionable feedback and push for a cleaner decomposition.\n\n## Review Tone\n\nBe direct, serious, and demanding about quality. Do not be rude, but do not soften major maintainability issues into mild suggestions. If the code is making the codebase messier, say so. If the implementation missed an obvious dramatic simplification, say that too. Do not be satisfied with \"maybe rename this\" when the real issue is structural.\n\n\n## Instructions\n\nReview the code through every lens in the rubric and the code-quality lens above that you find relevant. Do not force lenses that don't apply. A simple bug fix does not need paragraphs about architectural integrity.\n\nFor each finding, provide:\n\n1. **Severity**: `critical` | `warning` | `nit`\n   - `critical`: Would cause bugs, data loss, security issues, or fundamentally broken behavior\n   - `warning`: Design concern, maintainability risk, or correctness issue that isn't immediately broken but will cause pain\n   - `nit`: Style, naming, minor improvement.\n2. **Finding**: What the problem is, in concrete terms. Reference specific lines/functions.\n3. **Evidence**: Why you believe this is a problem. Show your reasoning. Don't just assert.\n4. **Suggestion** (optional): What you'd do instead, if you have a concrete alternative. Skip this if you don't have a clear fix.\n\n## What Makes a Good Finding\n\n- It references specific code, not vague concerns (\"this could be better\")\n- It explains WHY something is a problem, not just THAT it is\n- It distinguishes between \"this is broken\" and \"I would have done this differently\"\n- It considers the stated intent. A finding that ignores the context of what's being built is a bad finding\n\n## What to Avoid\n\n- Restating what the code does without identifying a problem\n- Praising the code. You're an adversary, not a cheerleader. If you find nothing wrong, say \"no findings\" and stop.\n\n## Output\n\nReturn your findings as a structured list. If you have zero findings, say so. An empty review is a valid outcome.\n\n```\n## Findings\n\n### 1. [Severity] Short title\n**Location**: file:line or function name\n**Finding**: What's wrong\n**Evidence**: Why this matters\n**Suggestion**: (optional) What to do instead\n\n### 2. [Severity] Short title\n...\n```\n"

      "path": "fixtures/cases/adversarial-review/outputs/reviewer-a/result.md",

      "sha256": "ced96449f446d86e2ec726aa5ae41c90b63651db4991a3d0e64aea38ab28e846",
      "owner": "local-013-initial-adversarial-review",
      "kind": "case-owned-reviewed-artifact",
      "text": "## Findings\n\n### 1. [critical] Immediate deletion breaks the queued disposal callback\n**Location**: cache.mjs:4 (invalidate), caller.mjs:5-6, vendor/disposer-v1.mjs:3\n**Finding**: Unmount now removes the entry before the disposal callback reads it. The supplied normal teardown path throws instead of reporting readiness, violating the requirement to preserve disposal correctness.\n**Evidence**: mount stores { ready: true } and installs a callback that evaluates get(key).ready. The returned unmount function calls subscription.dispose(), which queues onDispose as a microtask and returns without invoking it. invalidate(key) then synchronously deletes the map entry in the same call stack. Once that stack completes, the queued callback runs: get(key) returns undefined, so accessing .ready throws TypeError before report can be called. Previously the deletion was scheduled as a timer, allowing this queued microtask to read the entry first. The probe exercises this exact call chain and is written to exit with code 1 when teardown throws; that outcome is inferred from the supplied implementation, not an observed execution result.\n**Suggestion**: Preserve immediate cache deletion while making disposal independent of future cache lookup. In mount, create the entry as a local value, put that same value in the cache, and capture it in the disposal callback (for example, () => report(entry.ready)). This gives disposal access to its own mounted resource after cache removal and avoids relying on scheduling delays. Verify both that get(key) is undefined immediately after unmount and that the queued disposal callback reports true without throwing.\n\n## Scope and limits\n\nReviewed only the supplied reviewer-task.md evidence at input revision b437182f726bdb7cf322a783c5fbd947d9ab3214. Considered correctness, disposal ownership and state lifetime, root cause, structure, verification, complexity, security, and the code quality lens. No separate structural or security finding is supported by this small change. No other source files, reviewer output, memory, or external services were consulted; no code was executed and no fixes were applied. Dependency conclusions apply only to the exact embedded vendored fixture, not a public package.\n"

    {
      "path": "fixtures/cases/adversarial-review/outputs/reviewer-b/result.md",
      "bytes": 2296,
      "sha256": "dd4f1ef44eab28ee04d27972949a06182667e09d2a53bc4e577ae5e2566afd59",
      "owner": "local-013-initial-adversarial-review",
      "kind": "case-owned-reviewed-artifact",
      "text": "## Findings\n\n### 1. [critical] Immediate deletion breaks the deferred disposal callback\n**Location**: cache.mjs:4 (invalidate), with caller.mjs:5-6 and vendor/disposer-v1.mjs:3\n**Finding**: The existing unmount path deletes the entry before the disposer reads it. A normal mount followed by unmount therefore throws a TypeError instead of reporting the disposed entry's ready state. The change achieves immediate deletion but fails the stated requirement to preserve disposal correctness.\n**Evidence**: mount(key, report) first stores { ready: true } and registers () => report(get(key).ready). Its returned teardown invokes subscription.dispose(), whose exact supplied implementation calls queueMicrotask(onDispose). This schedules the callback without running it. Teardown then invokes invalidate(key), which now synchronously removes the entry. Once the synchronous teardown finishes, the microtask invokes get(key).ready; get(key) is undefined and property access throws before report can run. In the supplied probe this reaches the uncaughtException listener and causes process.exitCode = 1. This is a deterministic ordering failure, not a timing-dependent race. With the removed setTimeout deletion, the already queued disposal microtask ran before the deletion timer.\n**Suggestion**: Preserve immediate cache deletion by making disposal independent of cache membership. In mount, create and store the entry in a local binding and have the disposal callback read that captured entry's ready property. This keeps the object available to its own disposal callback after its cache key is removed, and prevents a subsequent mount under the same key from substituting a different entry. Do not restore deferred invalidation or add optional chaining that silently changes the report to undefined.\n\n## Review limits\n\nReviewed the entire supplied rubric and code-quality lens against the embedded after-state, diff, real caller, pinned local disposer, and probe. The finding is established by execution-order reasoning. No scripts exercising the implementation or external services were run; the probe outcome above is inferred, not observed. No public-package behavior is claimed. No additional independent structural, complexity, type, or security finding was supported by the supplied evidence.\n"

      "text": "# adversarial-review\n\n## Execution record\n\nOnly this single case was requested in the dedicated rerun. Native independent workers were used; no grading program was run and no outcome grade is assigned here.\n\n- Observed start: 2026-10-16 18:31:55 UTC, 21:31:55 Asia/Amman. Deadline: 18:43:55 UTC, 21:43:55 Asia/Amman.\n- Task repository HEAD verified before dispatch: `b437182f726bdb7cf322a783c5fbd947d9ab3214`.\n- Installed package `plugin.json` reports version `0.1.3`. Operative freeze supplied by user: `51f209daf83cd97d64f89881d66533bb59dc57db`. Package directory is a frozen cache without its own Git metadata; its mapping to that commit is supplied provenance, not an independently observed Git HEAD.\n- Main report revision supplied by user: `84e8ad3efc9368db8da3b192ca139f8718ea9912`, distinct from both task input and operative freeze. No main-report or prior-attempt outputs were read.\n- All writes are under this cwd's `outputs/`. No commits, external actions or fix were made. Before-state comparison copies restore only the removed timer line, under each reviewer's owned output; they are not treatment commits or proposed fixes.\n\n## Supplied input\n\n“Adversarially review the current changes against their stated intent. Challenge correctness and structural quality, then decide which findings warrant action.”\n\nSupplement: change deferred invalidation to immediate deletion while preserving disposal correctness; give two isolated reviewers the identical diff, pinned caller/dependency, full template, rubric and code-quality lens at the exact input SHA; dispatch both before reading either terminal result; classify every finding; do not patch or open a PR.\n\nFull case contract: `../task-contract.json`; read content captured in `raw/coordinator-initial.json`. Full filled reviewer input: `reviewer-common.md`, SHA-256 `2f9b5f59a6fadcc4a00c3cf7267cfccdb57d9d9afeaf6f3fc69bdb9399379cee`.\n\nThe reviewed diff is the supplied `invalidation.diff`, not an uncommitted Git diff. The repository itself started clean. The after-state cache, actual caller, manifest, exact vendored dependency and probe were copied from `committed-inputs/shared/additional/cache/` into `working/cache/` before any comparison edits. `verification.json` records all seven matching source/copy hashes.\n\n## Intent\n\nChange deferred invalidation to immediate deletion while preserving disposal correctness. Immediate invalidation must remove the cache entry now, while the mounted subscription's deferred teardown must still report its readiness without throwing.\n\n## Reviewers\n\n| Reviewer | Native worker | Requested model / effort | Observed model / effort | Findings |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| A | `/root/reviewer_a` | inherit-parent / inherit-parent | unknown / unknown | 1 critical, 0 warning, 0 nit |\n| B | `/root/reviewer_b` | inherit-parent / inherit-parent | unknown / unknown | 1 critical, 0 warning, 0 nit |\n\nBoth fresh-context workers used `fork_turns: none`, exclusive output paths, the same input SHA and common prompt digest. They each verified the SHA and digest, read all five exact frozen sourceFiles through tools, and returned complete artifacts. Requested inheritance is not an observed model identity. Worker independence is supported by distinct native dispatches and inspected results; model diversity remains unverified. This was not sequential role switching.\n\nObserved order: immutable A/B assignments and common input persisted; `collaboration.spawn_agent` A returned `/root/reviewer_a`; `collaboration.spawn_agent` B returned `/root/reviewer_b`; only then were terminal outputs read. A's full result was inspected before B's. Neither reviewer received the other's findings. Native terminal notifications and `collaboration.list_agents` provided worker state; actual review files and receipts, rather than notifications alone, were inspected. Assignment status remains immutable `pending`; terminal state is recorded separately in events/receipts. B's self-written receipt says `complete`; the coordinator records the terminal event as `completed` without rewriting that receipt.\n\n## Act On\n\n### F1. Critical: immediate deletion breaks deferred disposal\n\nRaised independently by A finding 1 and B finding 1. Accepted by the lead as a correctness blocker.\n\n`cache.mjs:4` synchronously deletes the entry. In `caller.mjs:5-6`, unmount calls `subscription.dispose()` and then `invalidate(key)`. Exact pinned `vendor/disposer-v1.mjs:3` queues the disposal callback with `queueMicrotask`; it does not run it synchronously. When that microtask later evaluates `get(key).ready`, the entry is already absent. The callback throws before `report` runs. This is deterministic ordering on the ordinary supplied mount/unmount path, rather than a hypothetical concurrent-input concern.\n\nBoth reviewer-owned after-state probes exited 1 with:\n\n```text\nteardown error: TypeError: undefined is not an object (evaluating 'get(key).ready')\n```\n\nBoth comparison probes restoring only deferred deletion exited 0 with:\n\n```text\ndisposed ready: true\nteardown completed\n```\n\nThe lead's separate unchanged after-state probe also exited 1 with the same error. Dependency SHA-256 is `d33e8d36600ad5df008657e478e39f9100a347098b4176f37923e5056cb3427f`, matching the fixture manifest and both reviewers' observations.\n\nRecommendation: retain the mounted entry directly in the disposal closure and migrate the caller alongside immediate deletion. That removes the hidden dependence on cache membership lasting through teardown. Verify immediate cache absence and successful deferred reporting together. This recommendation was not implemented or tested as a fix. Optional chaining would hide the exception while losing the required readiness value. Restoring the timer proves the regression relationship but does not satisfy the new immediate-deletion intent.\n\n## Consider\n\nNo separate findings. Both suggestions about retaining the entry are remediation for F1, not additional defects. A large abstraction or disposal framework is not justified by this small fixture.\n\n## Noted\n\nBoth reviews mention repeated disposal and overlapping same-key lifetimes as broader concerns; neither establishes another regression introduced by this diff. Lead classification: noted context only. These are not counted as additional reviewer findings. Evidence is confined to the exact local vendored implementation under Bun 1.4.2; no claim about a public package or other runtime is made.\n\n## Dismissed\n\nNo submitted finding was dismissed. The lead rejects treating the root-cause integration gap, callback exception and missing report as three separate findings: they share the same lifetime-contract defect F1. No speculative security, type, file-size or style finding is manufactured to fill this category.\n\n## Agreement Map\n\n| Deduplicated item | A | B | Lead decision | Rationale |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| F1 deferred callback reads deleted entry | A1 critical | B1 critical | Act On | Full call-chain evidence and actual before/after probe results establish regression |\n| Capture mounted entry directly | Suggestion on A1 | Suggestion on B1 | Remediation attached to F1 | Preserves immediate deletion while removing lookup lifetime coupling; not executed |\n| Repeated disposal / same-key overlap | Context, no separate finding | Context, no separate finding | Noted | Additional change-specific defect not established |\n\nTwo raw findings become one deduplicated finding. Independent worker agreement: F1, two of two. Lone findings: none. Explicit disagreements between reviewers: none. Lead agrees with both on F1. This supports worker agreement only; unknown actual models cannot establish multi-model consensus or diversity.\n\nAll rubric lenses were supplied verbatim: correctness, root causes versus symptoms, structural integrity, verification, complexity budget and security. All eight code-quality dimensions plus core prompt, output expectations, approval bar and tone were supplied verbatim. Both full reviews considered applicable lenses and found no additional concrete structural, boundary, type, size or security defect. This review is about actual native execution in this session, not host behavioral parity.\n\n## Variants, operations and limits\n\n| Requested or attempted variant | Actual observation | Evidence |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Concurrent independent inherited reviewers | Both native spawn calls succeeded before either terminal read; both complete results inspected | `assignment-a.json`, `assignment-b.json`, `raw/dispatch.json`, `raw/result-inspection-a.json`, `raw/result-inspection-b.json` |\n| Full template, caller and pinned dependency at one SHA | Common prompt and dependency digests matched for both workers | `input-manifest.json`, `verification.json`, worker receipts |\n| Unchanged after-state execution | Lead and both reviewers ran Bun; each exit 1 with actual teardown TypeError | `raw/coordinator-execution.json`, A `raw/probe-tool-records.json`, B `raw/operations.json` |\n| Reconstructed before-state comparison | Each reviewer restored only removed timer in owned copy; each Bun probe exit 0 | `reviewer-a/before/`, `reviewer-b/before/`, worker raw probe records |\n| Model diversity | Actual model names not exposed; unknown | Native spawn returns and both receipts |\n| Sequential fallback | Not attempted because independent workers were available | No sequential consensus claimed |\n| Negative authorization/service variants | No such variant requested; external writes prohibited and not attempted | No actual denial or external success is claimed |\n| Fix/PR | Not performed, as instructed | Input preservation check |\n\nGeneral tool discovery inspected `ALL_TOOLS` and the actual separate `collaboration` namespace. `ALL_TOOLS` did not contain the native collaboration operations; `collaboration.list_agents` observed the root and native `collaboration.spawn_agent` worked. No inference of absence was made from discovery listing alone. No model override/default repair or dependency install was attempted.\n\nActual errors retained: initial `rg --files` found no AGENTS.md and made its combined discovery command exit 1 after successful HEAD/status checks; a guessed frozen `.claude-plugin/plugin.json` path was absent (exit 1), then actual root `plugin.json` was read successfully; trying to serialize all tool descriptions into a shell argument failed with `Argument list too long (os error 7)`, then a bounded names-only capture succeeded. Some combined inspection displays were truncated; full review contents and relevant probe returns were subsequently inspected in smaller calls. The actual probe failures are retained, not normalized into service successes.\n\nRaw JSON includes exact command arguments/returns for worker reads/probes and principal coordinator observations. Native session tool history retains the original calls. Some early coordinator observations were persisted as summaries/content captures rather than a complete raw transcript; do not treat this artifact set as a byte-complete export of every session tool exchange. Worker B likewise discloses repeated source reads to obtain durable raw capture. No captures, worker identities, model names or measurements were fabricated.\n\nAll plugin skill/support reads stayed inside `/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3`. Source contents are in `raw/frozen-source-reads.json`; exact five source paths plus three frozen support documents are listed in `input-manifest.json`. Workers' actual frozen read calls/returns are retained in their raw directories. No unrelated memory, personal files, other case outputs or prior grades were read.\n\nOpen predicates: actual model identities/diversity unknown; frozen cache-to-Git mapping is supplied provenance; suggested caller fix remains unimplemented and unverified; no claim for another runtime or public dependency. The review and evidence persistence finish within the deadline; final timing and preservation readback are in `raw/final-verification.json`.\n\n## Artifact index\n\n- `reviewer-common.md`: identical complete reviewer input.\n- `assignment-a.json`, `assignment-b.json`: immutable pre-dispatch assignments.\n- `raw/dispatch.json`: native dispatch arguments/returns and declared/observed order.\n- `reviewer-a/review.md`, `reviewer-b/review.md`: full independent outputs.\n- `reviewer-a/receipt.json`, `reviewer-b/receipt.json`: revision, source-read and execution receipts.\n- `reviewer-a/raw/`, `reviewer-b/raw/`: worker actual tool evidence.\n- `raw/coordinator-initial.json`, `raw/coordinator-execution.json`, `raw/result-inspection-a.json`, `raw/result-inspection-b.json`: coordinator observations.\n- `input-manifest.json`, `verification.json`: dependency/common/copy/result hashes.\n- `raw/final-verification.json`: durable readback, final HEAD/status and completion time.\n"

    {
      "path": "reruns/local-01/adversarial-review/outputs/reviewer-b/review.md",
      "bytes": 3407,
      "sha256": "e18b60bd2749d543bab42fe39271ee8740fd31079164bfce48935ab17087935c",

      "kind": "case-owned-reviewed-artifact",
      "text": "## Findings\n\n### 1. [critical] Immediate deletion breaks every normal unmount's deferred disposal callback\n**Location**: outputs/working/cache/cache.mjs:4; dependent call chain outputs/working/cache/caller.mjs:5-6 and outputs/working/cache/vendor/disposer-v1.mjs:3.\n\n**Finding**: The new synchronous `invalidate(key)` deletes the entry before the disposal callback reads it. Calling `subscription.dispose()` first does not finish disposal: the exact vendored implementation queues the callback as a microtask. A normal unmount therefore raises an uncaught TypeError and never invokes `report`, violating the explicit requirement to preserve disposal correctness.\n\n**Evidence**: `mount` inserts an entry, then creates a callback evaluating `get(key).ready`. Its returned teardown first queues that callback and then deletes the map entry synchronously. After the teardown stack returns, the microtask runs, `get(key)` returns undefined, and dereferencing `ready` throws. This is deterministic ordering, with no concurrent thread or unusual input required. The pinned dependency SHA-256 matches manifest.json exactly. Running the unchanged supplied probe from an owned after-state copy with Bun 1.4.2 exits 1 and prints:\n\n```text\nteardown error: TypeError: undefined is not an object (evaluating 'get(key).ready')\n```\n\nA separate owned before-state copy, changing only the removed `setTimeout` line back, exits 0 and prints:\n\n```text\ndisposed ready: true\nteardown completed\n```\n\nRaw commands, complete tool returns, and exit statuses are in `raw/operations.json`. No fixture implementation was substituted for the vendored dependency.\n\n**Suggestion**: Keep immediate cache deletion, but migrate the caller so its disposal callback retains the mounted entry directly rather than looking it up through a cache whose lifetime has ended. Capture the object passed to `put` and read that object in the callback. This removes the hidden dependency on deletion timing without adding another timer or silently dropping `report`. Verify both immediate absence from `get` and a successful deferred disposal report on the same teardown.\n\n## Scope and lens coverage\n\nReviewed the complete common prompt, diff, after-state implementation, actual dependent caller, exact locally pinned disposer, manifest and supplied probe. Correctness, state lifetime, async ordering, error propagation, root cause, integration verification and structural ownership all lead to the finding above; these are one underlying defect, not separate findings. Idempotency and same-key overlap expose broader lifetime concerns, but the supplied diff does not establish an additional independent regression. No security input-to-sink path, file-size growth, ad-hoc branching, type casts, unused compatibility layer, or unnecessary abstraction was introduced. The useful structural simplification is to remove the disposal callback's dependence on a global cache lookup; no large refactor is warranted by this fixture.\n\n## Limitations\n\nEvidence applies to this exact local vendored fixture, not a public dependency or another version. Execution used Bun 1.4.2 only. The reconstructed before-state is a comparison copy derived from the supplied diff. No fix was applied, no external service was contacted, and no other reviewer output was read. Requested model: inherit-parent; actual model: unknown because the worker interface did not expose it.\n\n"
    },
