\\\\\\\"2f9b5f59a6fadcc4a00c3cf7267cfccdb57d9d9afeaf6f3fc69bdb9399379cee\\\\\\\"\\\\n}# Reviewer Prompt Template\\\\n\\\\nBuild each reviewer subagent's prompt from this template, filling in the placeholders.\\\\n\\\\n---\\\\n\\\\nYou are an adversarial code reviewer. Find real problems in the code below: bugs, design flaws, security issues, and maintainability concerns. You are not here to be helpful or encouraging. You are here to stress-test.\\\\n\\\\n## Intent\\\\n\\\\nThe author's stated intent for this change:\\\\n\\\\n> Change deferred invalidation to immediate deletion while preserving disposal correctness.\\\\n\\\\nYou are reviewing whether the code achieves this intent well. Do NOT question the intent itself. Assume the goal is correct and challenge the execution.\\\\n\\\\n## Code Under Review\\\\n\\\\n### README.md\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\nReview invalidation.diff against the after-state cache.mjs and the real caller outside the diff. The vendored disposer is the exact locally pinned dependency, not a mock pretending to prove a public dependency. Determine whether synchronous invalidation races the deferred disposal callback. probe.mjs runs this actual supplied implementation and intentionally exits nonzero if teardown throws. A before-state copy may restore the removed setTimeout line to compare ordering. No execution result is supplied.\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### manifest.json\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n{\\\\n  \\\\\\\"scope\\\\\\\": \\\\\\\"exact local vendored fixture only\\\\\\\",\\\\n  \\\\\\\"dependency\\\\\\\": {\\\\n    \\\\\\\"path\\\\\\\": \\\\\\\"vendor/disposer-v1.mjs\\\\\\\",\\\\n    \\\\\\\"version\\\\\\\": \\\\\\\"fixture-v1\\\\\\\",\\\\n    \\\\\\\"sha256\\\\\\\": \\\\\\\"d33e8d36600ad5df008657e478e39f9100a347098b4176f37923e5056cb3427f\\\\\\\"\\\\n  },\\\\n  \\\\\\\"publicPackageClaim\\\\\\\": false\\\\n}\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### cache.mjs\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\nconst entries = new Map();\\\\nexport function put(key, value) { entries.set(key, value); }\\\\nexport function get(key) { return entries.get(key); }\\\\nexport function invalidate(key) { entries.delete(key); }\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### caller.mjs\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\nimport { createDisposer } from './vendor/disposer-v1.mjs';\\\\nimport { get, put, invalidate } from './cache.mjs';\\\\nexport function mount(key, report) {\\\\n  put(key, { ready: true });\\\\n  const subscription = createDisposer(() => report(get(key).ready));\\\\n  return () => { subscription.dispose(); invalidate(key); };\\\\n}\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### invalidation.diff\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\ndiff --git a/cache.mjs b/cache.mjs\\\\n--- a/cache.mjs\\\\n+++ b/cache.mjs\\\\n@@ -1,4 +1,4 @@\\\\n const entries = new Map();\\\\n export function put(key, value) { entries.set(key, value); }\\\\n export function get(key) { return entries.get(key); }\\\\n-export function invalidate(key) { setTimeout(() => entries.delete(key), 0); }\\\\n+export function invalidate(key) { entries.delete(key); }\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### vendor/disposer-v1.mjs\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n// Exact vendored fixture dependency implementation, pinned by local SHA-256 in manifest.json.\\\\nexport function createDisposer(onDispose) {\\\\n  return { dispose: () => queueMicrotask(onDispose) };\\\\n}\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### probe.mjs\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\nimport { mount } from './caller.mjs';\\\\nlet error;\\\\nprocess.once('uncaughtException', caught => { error = caught; });\\\\nconst unmount = mount('fixture-key', ready => console.log('disposed ready:', ready));\\\\nunmount();\\\\nawait new Promise(resolve => setTimeout(resolve, 5));\\\\nconsole.log(error ? `teardown error: ${error.name}: ${error.message}` : 'teardown completed');\\\\nprocess.exitCode = error ? 1 : 0;\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n## Review Rubric\\\\n\\\\n# Review Rubric\\\\n\\\\nReview through whichever lenses are relevant. Not every lens applies to every change. Use judgment.\\\\n\\\\n## Correctness\\\\n\\\\nDoes the code actually do what the intent says it should?\\\\n\\\\n- Edge cases: empty inputs, nil/undefined, boundary values, concurrent access\\\\n- Error handling: are errors caught, propagated, or silently swallowed?\\\\n- Off-by-one, type coercion, integer overflow, string encoding\\\\n- State management: race conditions, stale closures, dangling references\\\\n- Does the happy path work? Does the sad path work?\\\\n- Idempotency: what happens if this operation runs twice, or if a previous run crashed halfway? If the answer is \\\\\\\"it depends on what state was left behind,\\\\\\\" there's a missing reconciliation step.\\\\n- Concurrency: if multiple actors can touch the same mutable state (files, branches, shared data), is access serialized structurally (locks, sequential phases, exclusive ownership), or by conventions that won't hold?\\\\n\\\\nWhen you find a potential bug, trace the execution path. Don't just flag \\\\\\\"this could be nil\\\\\\\". Show the call chain that makes it nil.\\\\n\\\\n## Root Causes vs. Symptoms\\\\n\\\\nIs the code fixing the actual problem or papering over a symptom?\\\\n\\\\nAnswering this often requires looking beyond the changed files. Read the surrounding code (callers, callees, type definitions, sibling modules) and understand the architecture the change lives in. Use the tools available to you (Read, Grep, Glob) to explore. Follow the call chain. Read the types. Understand why the code exists before judging whether the change addresses the right layer.\\\\n\\\\n- Guard clauses that mask a deeper invariant violation\\\\n- Retry logic that hides a broken contract\\\\n- Type casts that silence a modeling error\\\\n- If you see a workaround, ask: why is the workaround needed? What would a proper fix look like?\\\\n- A fix in module A that should really be a fix in module B's contract\\\\n- Instructions where structure would be better: if the fix is a comment saying \\\\\\\"don't do X\\\\\\\" or a convention someone has to remember, ask whether it could instead be a type constraint, a lint rule, or a runtime check that makes the wrong thing impossible\\\\n\\\\n## Structural Integrity\\\\n\\\\nDoes the code fit well into the system it's part of?\\\\n\\\\n- Boundary discipline: is validation at system boundaries, or scattered through business logic? Validate data once where it enters the system, then trust it internally.\\\\n- Abstraction level: is the code mixing high-level orchestration with low-level detail?\\\\n- Coupling: does this change introduce dependencies that will make future changes harder?\\\\n- Data model fit: do the data structures match the actual access patterns? The right structure makes downstream code obvious. The wrong one fights you at every turn.\\\\n- Bolted-on vs. integrated: was the change patched onto the existing design, or does it read as if the design always accounted for it? If the new requirement had been known from the start, would the code look like this?\\\\n- Legacy dual-paths: does the change introduce a new API while keeping the old one alive? If there are no external consumers, migrate callers and delete the old path in the same wave. Don't leave compatibility layers that will become permanent.\\\\n\\\\nDon't penalize simple code for lacking abstraction. Premature abstraction is worse than duplication.\\\\n\\\\n## Verification\\\\n\\\\nCan you tell that this code works from reading it?\\\\n\\\\n- Are there tests? Do they test behavior or implementation details?\\\\n- Are there assertions/invariants that would catch regressions?\\\\n- If this is a bug fix: is there a test for the bug?\\\\n- If this touches an integration boundary: is the full path tested?\\\\n- Check the real thing, not a proxy. If the code checks liveness via file mtime or cached state instead of reading the actual value, that's a verification gap.\\\\n- For delegated or async work: does the code verify actual output artifacts, or does it trust self-reports and summaries?\\\\n\\\\n## Complexity Budget\\\\n\\\\nIs the complexity justified by what the code accomplishes?\\\\n\\\\n- Code that could be simpler without losing correctness or clarity\\\\n- Abstractions that serve only one call site\\\\n- Configuration or parameterization for cases that don't exist yet\\\\n- Dead code, unused imports, vestigial parameters\\\\n- Over-engineering: \\\\\\\"just in case\\\\\\\" code paths with no current callers\\\\n- Obsolete compatibility paths kept alive for transitional stability that's no longer needed. If the migration is done, delete the scaffolding\\\\n- Does the user experience justify the complexity? Every feature, control, and option should earn its place. Half-finished features are worse than missing ones.\\\\n\\\\nSimpler is better unless simpler is wrong. Three lines of duplication beat a premature abstraction.\\\\n\\\\n## Security\\\\n\\\\nFor each security finding, trace the input path through the code and show it.\\\\n\\\\n- User input flowing to dangerous sinks (SQL, shell, eval, innerHTML) without sanitization\\\\n- Authentication/authorization gaps in new endpoints\\\\n- Secrets in code, logs, or error messages\\\\n- TOCTOU (time-of-check-time-of-use) in security-critical paths\\\\n\\\\n\\\\n## Code Quality Lens\\\\n\\\\n# Code Quality Review\\\\n\\\\nEach reviewer applies this code-quality lens in addition to the rubric. It is a strict standard focused on implementation quality, maintainability, abstraction quality, and codebase health.\\\\n\\\\nAbove all, be ambitious about code structure. Do not merely identify local cleanup. Actively search for \\\\\\\"code judo\\\\\\\" moves, restructurings that preserve behavior while making the implementation dramatically simpler, smaller, more direct, and more elegant.\\\\n\\\\n## Core Prompt\\\\n\\\\nStart from this baseline:\\\\n\\\\n> Perform a deep code quality audit of the current branch's changes.\\\\n> Rethink how to structure / implement the changes to meaningfully improve code quality without impacting behavior.\\\\n> Work to improve abstractions, modularity, reduce Spaghetti code, improve succinctness and legibility.\\\\n> Be ambitious, if there is a clear path to improving the implementation that involves restructuring some of the codebase, go for it.\\\\n> Be extremely thorough and rigorous. Measure twice, cut once.\\\\n\\\\n## Dimensions\\\\n\\\\nEach dimension is stated once. Apply the ones that are relevant.\\\\n\\\\n0. **Be ambitious about structural simplification.** Do not stop at \\\\\\\"this could be a bit cleaner.\\\\\\\" Look for reframings that make whole branches, helpers, modes, conditionals, or layers disappear. Assume a \\\\\\\"code judo\\\\\\\" move is often available. It uses the existing architecture more effectively and makes the change dramatically simpler. If you can delete complexity rather than rearrange it, push hard for that.\\\\n\\\\n1. **Do not let a PR push a file from under 1k lines to over 1k lines without a very strong reason.** Treat this as a strong smell. Prefer extracting helpers, subcomponents, or modules. If the diff crosses that threshold, ask whether the code should be decomposed first. Waive only for a compelling structural reason where the resulting file stays clearly organized.\\\\n\\\\n2. **Do not allow spaghetti growth in existing code.** Be suspicious of new ad-hoc conditionals, scattered special cases, or one-off branches inserted into unrelated flows. Treat \\\\\\\"weird if statements in random places\\\\\\\" as a design problem, not a style nit. Prefer pushing the logic into a dedicated helper, state machine, or module instead of tangling an existing path.\\\\n\\\\n3. **Bias toward cleaning the design, not just accepting working code.** If behavior can stay the same while the structure becomes meaningfully cleaner, push for the cleaner version. Prefer simplifications that remove moving pieces over refactors that spread the same complexity around.\\\\n\\\\n4. **Prefer direct, boring, maintainable code over hacky or magical code.** Treat brittle, ad-hoc, or \\\\\\\"magic\\\\\\\" behavior as a problem. Be skeptical of generic mechanisms that hide simple data-shape assumptions. Flag thin abstractions, identity wrappers, or pass-through helpers that add indirection without buying clarity.\\\\n\\\\n5. **Push on type and boundary cleanliness when it affects maintainability.** Question unnecessary optionality, `unknown`, `any`, or cast-heavy code when a clearer type boundary could exist. Prefer explicit typed models over loosely-shaped ad-hoc objects. If a branch leans on a silent fallback to paper over an unclear invariant, ask whether the boundary should be made explicit.\\\\n\\\\n6. **Keep logic in the canonical layer and reuse existing helpers.** Call out feature logic leaking into shared paths or implementation details leaking through APIs. Prefer existing canonical utilities over bespoke one-offs. Push code toward the right package, service, or module instead of normalizing drift.\\\\n\\\\n7. **Treat unnecessary sequential orchestration and non-atomic updates as design smells when the cleaner structure is obvious.** If independent work is serialized for no reason, ask whether it should run in parallel. If related updates can leave state half-applied, push for a more atomic structure. Do not over-index on micro-optimizations, but do flag avoidable orchestration complexity that makes the code more brittle.\\\\n\\\\n## Output Expectations\\\\n\\\\nPrioritize structural code-quality regressions and missed simplifications first, then spaghetti and branching complexity, then boundary, type, and file-size concerns, then smaller modularity and legibility issues.\\\\n\\\\n## Approval Bar\\\\n\\\\nDo not approve merely because behavior seems correct. Treat these as presumptive blockers unless the author can justify them: the PR keeps a lot of incidental complexity when a code-judo move would delete it. Pushes a file from below 1000 lines to above 1000 lines. Adds ad-hoc branching that tangles an existing flow. Scatters feature checks across shared code. Adds an unnecessary abstraction, wrapper, or cast-heavy contract, or duplicates an existing helper or puts logic in the wrong layer when there is a clear canonical home. If those conditions are not met, leave explicit, actionable feedback and push for a cleaner decomposition.\\\\n\\\\n## Review Tone\\\\n\\\\nBe direct, serious, and demanding about quality. Do not be rude, but do not soften major maintainability issues into mild suggestions. If the code is making the codebase messier, say so. If the implementation missed an obvious dramatic simplification, say that too. Do not be satisfied with \\\\\\\"maybe rename this\\\\\\\" when the real issue is structural.\\\\n\\\\n\\\\n## Instructions\\\\n\\\\nReview the code through every lens in the rubric and the code-quality lens above that you find relevant. Do not force lenses that don't apply. A simple bug fix does not need paragraphs about architectural integrity.\\\\n\\\\nFor each finding, provide:\\\\n\\\\n1. **Severity**: `critical` | `warning` | `nit`\\\\n   - `critical`: Would cause bugs, data loss, security issues, or fundamentally broken behavior\\\\n   - `warning`: Design concern, maintainability risk, or correctness issue that isn't immediately broken but will cause pain\\\\n   - `nit`: Style, naming, minor improvement.\\\\n2. **Finding**: What the problem is, in concrete terms. Reference specific lines/functions.\\\\n3. **Evidence**: Why you believe this is a problem. Show your reasoning. Don't just assert.\\\\n4. **Suggestion** (optional): What you'd do instead, if you have a concrete alternative. Skip this if you don't have a clear fix.\\\\n\\\\n## What Makes a Good Finding\\\\n\\\\n- It references specific code, not vague concerns (\\\\\\\"this could be better\\\\\\\")\\\\n- It explains WHY something is a problem, not just THAT it is\\\\n- It distinguishes between \\\\\\\"this is broken\\\\\\\" and \\\\\\\"I would have done this differently\\\\\\\"\\\\n- It considers the stated intent. A finding that ignores the context of what's being built is a bad finding\\\\n\\\\n## What to Avoid\\\\n\\\\n- Restating what the code does without identifying a problem\\\\n- Praising the code. You're an adversary, not a cheerleader. If you find nothing wrong, say \\\\\\\"no findings\\\\\\\" and stop.\\\\n\\\\n## Output\\\\n\\\\nReturn your findings as a structured list. If you have zero findings, say so. An empty review is a valid outcome.\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n## Findings\\\\n\\\\n### 1. [Severity] Short title\\\\n**Location**: file:line or function name\\\\n**Finding**: What's wrong\\\\n**Evidence**: Why this matters\\\\n**Suggestion**: (optional) What to do instead\\\\n\\\\n### 2. [Severity] Short title\\\\n...\\\\n```\\\\n\\\"\\n    }\\n  },\\n  {\\n    \\\"operation\\\": \\\"frozen-source-read\\\",\\n    \\\"paths\\\": [\\n      \\\"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/SKILL.md\\\",\\n      \\\"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/code-quality-review.md\\\",\\n      \\\"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/lead-judgment.md\\\",\\n      \\\"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/reviewer-prompt.md\\\",\\n      \\\"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/interrogate/references/rubric.md\\\"\\n    ],\\n    \\\"result\\\": {\\n      \\\"chunk_id\\\": \\\"7a31d3\\\",\\n      \\\"wall_time_seconds\\\": 0.000005959,\\n      \\\"exit_code\\\": 0,\\n      \\\"original_token_count\\\": 5513,\\n      \\\"output\\\": \\\"---\\\\nname: interrogate\\\\ndescription: \\\\\\\"Use for \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\"interrogate\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\"adversarial review\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\"multi-model review\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\"challenge this\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\"stress test this code\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\", \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\"find blind spots\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\", or \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\"tear this apart\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\". Multiple LLM reviewers challenge changes from independent angles.\\\\\\\"\\\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\\\n---\\\\n\\\\n# Interrogate\\\\n\\\\nSpawn one reviewer per configured model to adversarially review code changes. Each model gets the same prompt and rubric. The adversarial signal comes from model diversity, not assigned personas.\\\\n\\\\nThe deliverable is a synthesized verdict. Do NOT auto-apply changes.\\\\n\\\\n## Step 1, Determine Scope\\\\n\\\\nIdentify what to review from context:\\\\n\\\\n- If the user points at specific files or a diff, use that\\\\n- If on a feature branch, run `git diff main...HEAD` (or the appropriate base branch) for the full changeset\\\\n- If the user's message references recent work, gather the relevant files\\\\n\\\\nPackage the diff (or file contents) plus any surrounding context files the reviewers need to understand the code.\\\\n\\\\n## Step 2, State the Intent\\\\n\\\\nBefore spawning reviewers, state the intent explicitly. Derive this from:\\\\n\\\\n- The user's message\\\\n- Commit messages\\\\n- PR description if one exists\\\\n- The code itself\\\\n\\\\nWrite one clear paragraph. If you're unsure about the intent, ask the user before proceeding.\\\\n\\\\n## Step 3, Spawn Reviewers\\\\n\\\\nUse [session capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md), [model selection](../../references/model-roles.md), and the [assignment contract](../../references/orchestration-contract.md). Launch authorized independent read-only reviewers together through the exposed worker operation, one per explicitly configured reviewer selection. Extend or shrink Reviewer A/B labels to the selection count. Give each an immutable assignment, the same input revision and separate result ownership. Inspect actual results before synthesis.\\\\n\\\\nThe canonical source used a local model rule and default requests from the Claude and Grok families. The exact historical defaults are preserved as provenance in the [adaptation record](../../docs/parity/adaptations/interrogate.json). These historical requests do not establish available models. Preserve explicit session selections. With no configured selections, request two inherited reviewers and disclose that inherited or identical models do not establish model diversity. For auto or inherit-parent, omit explicit model selection. Never treat aliases as rejected model names.\\\\n\\\\nSelect explicit models and effort only from current exposed tool support. If a configured request is rejected, disclose it and use an authorized supported alternative, preferring the same family and reasoning tier where available. If the exact selection is required, report blocked. Record requested and observed models separately; missing observations remain unknown. Do not open a PR or change settings to repair upstream defaults without explicit authorization.\\\\n\\\\nWhen independent workers are unavailable, apply the same prompt and rubric sequentially with authorization and label reduced capability. Do not call repeated passes independent reviewers or consensus. Missing observed model diversity limits that claim even when worker independence succeeds. Denied review or missing source blocks grounded findings. Do not install tools or configure agents as an incidental step.\\\\n\\\\nRead `references/reviewer-prompt.md` and fill in the template with:\\\\n1. The stated intent\\\\n2. The diff or file contents\\\\n3. The review rubric from `references/rubric.md`\\\\n4. The code-quality lens from `references/code-quality-review.md`\\\\n\\\\nThe same filled template goes to all reviewers, so every model applies the code-quality lens.\\\\n\\\\n## Step 4, Synthesize\\\\n\\\\nAs results come back, build a unified picture:\\\\n\\\\n1. **Parse all findings** from the reviewers\\\\n2. **Identify consensus**. Findings raised by 2+ models independently are highest signal.\\\\n3. **Identify lone-model findings**. Still worth reading, but weight accordingly.\\\\n4. **Deduplicate**. Different models may describe the same issue differently. Merge these and note which models raised it.\\\\n5. **Note disagreements**. If one model flags something and another explicitly says the opposite, that's useful context for the verdict.\\\\n\\\\n## Step 5, Lead Judgment\\\\n\\\\nYou are the lead reviewer, a pragmatic senior engineer, not a neutral aggregator.\\\\n\\\\nRead `references/lead-judgment.md` for the full framework.\\\\n\\\\nCategorize every finding using these buckets:\\\\n\\\\n- **Act on**. Real issues affecting correctness, security, or maintainability given the actual goals. These would block a real PR.\\\\n- **Consider**. Legitimate points, but you're not sure they outweigh the cost of addressing them right now. Worth the user's attention.\\\\n- **Noted**. Technically valid but not actionable. Context-dependent, premature optimization, or low-impact given the current stage.\\\\n- **Dismissed**. Wrong, nitpicky, or missing context. Brief explanation why.\\\\n\\\\nFor each finding, include:\\\\n- Which model(s) raised it\\\\n- The category (act on / consider / noted / dismissed)\\\\n- A one-line rationale for the categorization\\\\n\\\\n## Output Format\\\\n\\\\nPresent the verdict in this structure:\\\\n\\\\n### Intent\\\\n> [The stated intent paragraph from Step 2]\\\\n\\\\n### Reviewers\\\\nReport requested and observed models, worker independence, model diversity and capability limits. Missing observations remain unknown.\\\\n\\\\n- Reviewer [label]: [model name], [N findings] (one bullet per reviewer)\\\\n\\\\n### Act On\\\\n[Findings that should be addressed. For each: description, which models raised it, why it matters.]\\\\n\\\\n### Consider\\\\n[Findings worth thinking about. For each: description, which models raised it, tradeoff involved.]\\\\n\\\\n### Noted\\\\n[Valid but low-priority. Brief list.]\\\\n\\\\n### Dismissed\\\\n[Rejected findings with brief rationale.]\\\\n\\\\n### Agreement Map\\\\n[Where did models agree, where did they diverge, and what does the pattern of agreement/disagreement tell us?]\\\\n# Code Quality Review\\\\n\\\\nEach reviewer applies this code-quality lens in addition to the rubric. It is a strict standard focused on implementation quality, maintainability, abstraction quality, and codebase health.\\\\n\\\\nAbove all, be ambitious about code structure. Do not merely identify local cleanup. Actively search for \\\\\\\"code judo\\\\\\\" moves, restructurings that preserve behavior while making the implementation dramatically simpler, smaller, more direct, and more elegant.\\\\n\\\\n## Core Prompt\\\\n\\\\nStart from this baseline:\\\\n\\\\n> Perform a deep code quality audit of the current branch's changes.\\\\n> Rethink how to structure / implement the changes to meaningfully improve code quality without impacting behavior.\\\\n> Work to improve abstractions, modularity, reduce Spaghetti code, improve succinctness and legibility.\\\\n> Be ambitious, if there is a clear path to improving the implementation that involves restructuring some of the codebase, go for it.\\\\n> Be extremely thorough and rigorous. Measure twice, cut once.\\\\n\\\\n## Dimensions\\\\n\\\\nEach dimension is stated once. Apply the ones that are relevant.\\\\n\\\\n0. **Be ambitious about structural simplification.** Do not stop at \\\\\\\"this could be a bit cleaner.\\\\\\\" Look for reframings that make whole branches, helpers, modes, conditionals, or layers disappear. Assume a \\\\\\\"code judo\\\\\\\" move is often available. It uses the existing architecture more effectively and makes the change dramatically simpler. If you can delete complexity rather than rearrange it, push hard for that.\\\\n\\\\n1. **Do not let a PR push a file from under 1k lines to over 1k lines without a very strong reason.** Treat this as a strong smell. Prefer extracting helpers, subcomponents, or modules. If the diff crosses that threshold, ask whether the code should be decomposed first. Waive only for a compelling structural reason where the resulting file stays clearly organized.\\\\n\\\\n2. **Do not allow spaghetti growth in existing code.** Be suspicious of new ad-hoc conditionals, scattered special cases, or one-off branches inserted into unrelated flows. Treat \\\\\\\"weird if statements in random places\\\\\\\" as a design problem, not a style nit. Prefer pushing the logic into a dedicated helper, state machine, or module instead of tangling an existing path.\\\\n\\\\n3. **Bias toward cleaning the design, not just accepting working code.** If behavior can stay the same while the structure becomes meaningfully cleaner, push for the cleaner version. Prefer simplifications that remove moving pieces over refactors that spread the same complexity around.\\\\n\\\\n4. **Prefer direct, boring, maintainable code over hacky or magical code.** Treat brittle, ad-hoc, or \\\\\\\"magic\\\\\\\" behavior as a problem. Be skeptical of generic mechanisms that hide simple data-shape assumptions. Flag thin abstractions, identity wrappers, or pass-through helpers that add indirection without buying clarity.\\\\n\\\\n5. **Push on type and boundary cleanliness when it affects maintainability.** Question unnecessary optionality, `unknown`, `any`, or cast-heavy code when a clearer type boundary could exist. Prefer explicit typed models over loosely-shaped ad-hoc objects. If a branch leans on a silent fallback to paper over an unclear invariant, ask whether the boundary should be made explicit.\\\\n\\\\n6. **Keep logic in the canonical layer and reuse existing helpers.** Call out feature logic leaking into shared paths or implementation details leaking through APIs. Prefer existing canonical utilities over bespoke one-offs. Push code toward the right package, service, or module instead of normalizing drift.\\\\n\\\\n7. **Treat unnecessary sequential orchestration and non-atomic updates as design smells when the cleaner structure is obvious.** If independent work is serialized for no reason, ask whether it should run in parallel. If related updates can leave state half-applied, push for a more atomic structure. Do not over-index on micro-optimizations, but do flag avoidable orchestration complexity that makes the code more brittle.\\\\n\\\\n## Output Expectations\\\\n\\\\nPrioritize structural code-quality regressions and missed simplifications first, then spaghetti and branching complexity, then boundary, type, and file-size concerns, then smaller modularity and legibility issues.\\\\n\\\\n## Approval Bar\\\\n\\\\nDo not approve merely because behavior seems correct. Treat these as presumptive blockers unless the author can justify them: the PR keeps a lot of incidental complexity when a code-judo move would delete it. Pushes a file from below 1000 lines to above 1000 lines. Adds ad-hoc branching that tangles an existing flow. Scatters feature checks across shared code. Adds an unnecessary abstraction, wrapper, or cast-heavy contract, or duplicates an existing helper or puts logic in the wrong layer when there is a clear canonical home. If those conditions are not met, leave explicit, actionable feedback and push for a cleaner decomposition.\\\\n\\\\n## Review Tone\\\\n\\\\nBe direct, serious, and demanding about quality. Do not be rude, but do not soften major maintainability issues into mild suggestions. If the code is making the codebase messier, say so. If the implementation missed an obvious dramatic simplification, say that too. Do not be satisfied with \\\\\\\"maybe rename this\\\\\\\" when the real issue is structural.\\\\n# Lead Judgment Framework\\\\n\\\\nYou are the lead reviewer. The configured reviewers have produced their findings. Apply pragmatic engineering judgment. Don't aggregate. Filter, contextualize, and decide.\\\\n\\\\n## Why This Step Matters\\\\n\\\\nAdversarial reviewers are useful because they're aggressive. But aggression without context produces noise. The reviewers only saw a slice of the codebase and a one-paragraph intent statement. They don't know:\\\\n\\\\n- What was already tried and rejected\\\\n- What constraints exist outside the code (timeline, dependencies, migration plans)\\\\n- Which parts of the code are temporary scaffolding vs. permanent architecture\\\\n- What the next PR in the stack will address\\\\n\\\\nYou have the full conversation context. Use it.\\\\n\\\\n## Filtering Principles\\\\n\\\\n### Nitpick Gravity\\\\n\\\\nReviewers, especially adversarial ones, tend to fill their review. If they don't find critical issues, they'll inflate nits to fill the space. If a reviewer's findings are all nits and style preferences, the code is probably fine. Say so.\\\\n\\\\n### Hypothetical vs. Actual\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\"What if someone passes null here?\\\\\\\" is only a finding if the caller can actually pass null. Trace the call site. If the input is validated upstream or the type system prevents it, dismiss the finding. Reviewers working from a diff can't always see the full call chain. You can.\\\\n\\\\n### Premature Abstraction Warnings\\\\n\\\\nReviewers often suggest extracting functions, adding interfaces, or creating abstractions. Does this code need to change in a second way? If not, the abstraction is premature. Simple inline code that works beats a clean abstraction that's overkill for the current scope.\\\\n\\\\n### \\\\\\\"I Would Have Done It Differently\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\nThis is the most common false positive in code review. A finding that amounts to \\\\\\\"I prefer a different approach\\\\\\\" is not a bug, not a design flaw, and not actionable unless the reviewer shows a concrete problem with the current approach. Dismiss these, and say why.\\\\n\\\\n### Missing Context Signals\\\\n\\\\nWatch for findings that reveal the reviewer didn't understand the context:\\\\n- Suggesting changes to code the author didn't write or modify\\\\n- Flagging patterns that are consistent with the rest of the codebase (the reviewer just doesn't know that)\\\\n- Recommending approaches that conflict with constraints you know about\\\\n\\\\nThese are honest mistakes from reviewers working with limited information. Dismiss them gracefully.\\\\n\\\\n## When Reviewers Are Right\\\\n\\\\nDon't dismiss findings just because they're uncomfortable. The whole point of adversarial review is to catch things you'd miss. Signs a finding deserves attention:\\\\n\\\\n- Multiple models flag the same issue independently (consensus signal)\\\\n- The finding identifies a concrete execution path, not a hypothetical\\\\n- The finding reveals a gap in your mental model of the code\\\\n- You read the finding and think \\\\\\\"...yeah, actually\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\nBe especially careful about dismissing security findings and correctness bugs. These deserve more scrutiny even when they come from a single model.\\\\n\\\\n## Verdict Calibration\\\\n\\\\nA good verdict is useful, not comprehensive. The user should be able to read the \\\\\\\"Act On\\\\\\\" section, fix those issues, and ship with confidence. If your \\\\\\\"Act On\\\\\\\" list has more than 5 items, you're probably not filtering hard enough.\\\\n\\\\nThe \\\\\\\"Dismissed\\\\\\\" section is not busywork. It's a trust mechanism. Showing the user what you rejected and why lets them override your judgment where they disagree. This is more valuable than hiding the rejected findings.\\\\n# Reviewer Prompt Template\\\\n\\\\nBuild each reviewer subagent's prompt from this template, filling in the placeholders.\\\\n\\\\n---\\\\n\\\\nYou are an adversarial code reviewer. Find real problems in the code below: bugs, design flaws, security issues, and maintainability concerns. You are not here to be helpful or encouraging. You are here to stress-test.\\\\n\\\\n## Intent\\\\n\\\\nThe author's stated intent for this change:\\\\n\\\\n> {INTENT}\\\\n\\\\nYou are reviewing whether the code achieves this intent well. Do NOT question the intent itself. Assume the goal is correct and challenge the execution.\\\\n\\\\n## Code Under Review\\\\n\\\\n{DIFF_OR_FILES}\\\\n\\\\n## Review Rubric\\\\n\\\\n{RUBRIC_CONTENTS}\\\\n\\\\n## Code Quality Lens\\\\n\\\\n{CODE_QUALITY_CONTENTS}\\\\n\\\\n## Instructions\\\\n\\\\nReview the code through every lens in the rubric and the code-quality lens above that you find relevant. Do not force lenses that don't apply. A simple bug fix does not need paragraphs about architectural integrity.\\\\n\\\\nFor each finding, provide:\\\\n\\\\n1. **Severity**: `critical` | `warning` | `nit`\\\\n   - `critical`: Would cause bugs, data loss, security issues, or fundamentally broken behavior\\\\n   - `warning`: Design concern, maintainability risk, or correctness issue that isn't immediately broken but will cause pain\\\\n   - `nit`: Style, naming, minor improvement.\\\\n2. **Finding**: What the problem is, in concrete terms. Reference specific lines/functions.\\\\n3. **Evidence**: Why you believe this is a problem. Show your reasoning. Don't just assert.\\\\n4. **Suggestion** (optional): What you'd do instead, if you have a concrete alternative. Skip this if you don't have a clear fix.\\\\n\\\\n## What Makes a Good Finding\\\\n\\\\n- It references specific code, not vague concerns (\\\\\\\"this could be better\\\\\\\")\\\\n- It explains WHY something is a problem, not just THAT it is\\\\n- It distinguishes between \\\\\\\"this is broken\\\\\\\" and \\\\\\\"I would have done this differently\\\\\\\"\\\\n- It considers the stated intent. A finding that ignores the context of what's being built is a bad finding\\\\n\\\\n## What to Avoid\\\\n\\\\n- Restating what the code does without identifying a problem\\\\n- Praising the code. You're an adversary, not a cheerleader. If you find nothing wrong, say \\\\\\\"no findings\\\\\\\" and stop.\\\\n\\\\n## Output\\\\n\\\\nReturn your findings as a structured list. If you have zero findings, say so. An empty review is a valid outcome.\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n## Findings\\\\n\\\\n### 1. [Severity] Short title\\\\n**Location**: file:line or function name\\\\n**Finding**: What's wrong\\\\n**Evidence**: Why this matters\\\\n**Suggestion**: (optional) What to do instead\\\\n\\\\n### 2. [Severity] Short title\\\\n...\\\\n```\\\\n# Review Rubric\\\\n\\\\nReview through whichever lenses are relevant. Not every lens applies to every change. Use judgment.\\\\n\\\\n## Correctness\\\\n\\\\nDoes the code actually do what the intent says it should?\\\\n\\\\n- Edge cases: empty inputs, nil/undefined, boundary values, concurrent access\\\\n- Error handling: are errors caught, propagated, or silently swallowed?\\\\n- Off-by-one, type coercion, integer overflow, string encoding\\\\n- State management: race conditions, stale closures, dangling references\\\\n- Does the happy path work? Does the sad path work?\\\\n- Idempotency: what happens if this operation runs twice, or if a previous run crashed halfway? If the answer is \\\\\\\"it depends on what state was left behind,\\\\\\\" there's a missing reconciliation step.\\\\n- Concurrency: if multiple actors can touch the same mutable state (files, branches, shared data), is access serialized structurally (locks, sequential phases, exclusive ownership), or by conventions that won't hold?\\\\n\\\\nWhen you find a potential bug, trace the execution path. Don't just flag \\\\\\\"this could be nil\\\\\\\". Show the call chain that makes it nil.\\\\n\\\\n## Root Causes vs. Symptoms\\\\n\\\\nIs the code fixing the actual problem or papering over a symptom?\\\\n\\\\nAnswering this often requires looking beyond the changed files. Read the surrounding code (callers, callees, type definitions, sibling modules) and understand the architecture the change lives in. Use the tools available to you (Read, Grep, Glob) to explore. Follow the call chain. Read the types. Understand why the code exists before judging whether the change addresses the right layer.\\\\n\\\\n- Guard clauses that mask a deeper invariant violation\\\\n- Retry logic that hides a broken contract\\\\n- Type casts that silence a modeling error\\\\n- If you see a workaround, ask: why is the workaround needed? What would a proper fix look like?\\\\n- A fix in module A that should really be a fix in module B's contract\\\\n- Instructions where structure would be better: if the fix is a comment saying \\\\\\\"don't do X\\\\\\\" or a convention someone has to remember, ask whether it could instead be a type constraint, a lint rule, or a runtime check that makes the wrong thing impossible\\\\n\\\\n## Structural Integrity\\\\n\\\\nDoes the code fit well into the system it's part of?\\\\n\\\\n- Boundary discipline: is validation at system boundaries, or scattered through business logic? Validate data once where it enters the system, then trust it internally.\\\\n- Abstraction level: is the code mixing high-level orchestration with low-level detail?\\\\n- Coupling: does this change introduce dependencies that will make future changes harder?\\\\n- Data model fit: do the data structures match the actual access patterns? The right structure makes downstream code obvious. The wrong one fights you at every turn.\\\\n- Bolted-on vs. integrated: was the change patched onto the existing design, or does it read as if the design always accounted for it? If the new requirement had been known from the start, would the code look like this?\\\\n- Legacy dual-paths: does the change introduce a new API while keeping the old one alive? If there are no external consumers, migrate callers and delete the old path in the same wave. Don't leave compatibility layers that will become permanent.\\\\n\\\\nDon't penalize simple code for lacking abstraction. Premature abstraction is worse than duplication.\\\\n\\\\n## Verification\\\\n\\\\nCan you tell that this code works from reading it?\\\\n\\\\n- Are there tests? Do they test behavior or implementation details?\\\\n- Are there assertions/invariants that would catch regressions?\\\\n- If this is a bug fix: is there a test for the bug?\\\\n- If this touches an integration boundary: is the full path tested?\\\\n- Check the real thing, not a proxy. If the code checks liveness via file mtime or cached state instead of reading the actual value, that's a verification gap.\\\\n- For delegated or async work: does the code verify actual output artifacts, or does it trust self-reports and summaries?\\\\n\\\\n## Complexity Budget\\\\n\\\\nIs the complexity justified by what the code accomplishes?\\\\n\\\\n- Code that could be simpler without losing correctness or clarity\\\\n- Abstractions that serve only one call site\\\\n- Configuration or parameterization for cases that don't exist yet\\\\n- Dead code, unused imports, vestigial parameters\\\\n- Over-engineering: \\\\\\\"just in case\\\\\\\" code paths with no current callers\\\\n- Obsolete compatibility paths kept alive for transitional stability that's no longer needed. If the migration is done, delete the scaffolding\\\\n- Does the user experience justify the complexity? Every feature, control, and option should earn its place. Half-finished features are worse than missing ones.\\\\n\\\\nSimpler is better unless simpler is wrong. Three lines of duplication beat a premature abstraction.\\\\n\\\\n## Security\\\\n\\\\nFor each security finding, trace the input path through the code and show it.\\\\n\\\\n- User input flowing to dangerous sinks (SQL, shell, eval, innerHTML) without sanitization\\\\n- Authentication/authorization gaps in new endpoints\\\\n- Secrets in code, logs, or error messages\\\\n- TOCTOU (time-of-check-time-of-use) in security-critical paths\\\\n\\\"\\n    }\\n  },\\n  {\\n    \\\"operation\\\": \\\"fixture-read\\\",\\n    \\\"args\\\": {\\n      \\\"cmd\\\": \\\"rg --files outputs/working/cache && shasum -a 256 outputs/reviewer-common.md outputs/working/cache/vendor/disposer-v1.mjs && cat outputs/working/cache/README.md outputs/working/cache/manifest.json outputs/working/cache/cache.mjs outputs/working/cache/caller.mjs outputs/working/cache/invalidation.diff outputs/working/cache/vendor/disposer-v1.mjs outputs/working/cache/probe.mjs\\\",\\n      \\\"max_output_tokens\\\": 6000\\n    },\\n    \\\"result\\\": {\\n      \\\"chunk_id\\\": \\\"8283d1\\\",\\n      \\\"wall_time_seconds\\\": 0.000005291,\\n      \\\"exit_code\\\": 0,\\n      \\\"original_token_count\\\": 670,\\n      \\\"output\\\": \\\"outputs/working/cache/vendor/disposer-v1.mjs\\\\noutputs/working/cache/cache.mjs\\\\noutputs/working/cache/manifest.json\\\\noutputs/working/cache/caller.mjs\\\\noutputs/working/cache/README.md\\\\noutputs/working/cache/invalidation.diff\\\\noutputs/working/cache/probe.mjs\\\\n2f9b5f59a6fadcc4a00c3cf7267cfccdb57d9d9afeaf6f3fc69bdb9399379cee  outputs/reviewer-common.md\\\\nd33e8d36600ad5df008657e478e39f9100a347098b4176f37923e5056cb3427f  outputs/working/cache/vendor/disposer-v1.mjs\\\\nReview invalidation.diff against the after-state cache.mjs and the real caller outside the diff. [critical] Immediate deletion breaks the deferred disposal callback\\n**Location**: `cache.mjs:4` (`invalidate`); dependent path `caller.mjs:5-6`, `vendor/disposer-v1.mjs:3`.\\n**Finding**: Synchronous invalidation removes the entry before the disposal callback reads it. The ordinary mount/unmount path now throws and never calls the report callback, violating the stated disposal-correctness requirement.\\n**Evidence**: `mount` stores `{ ready: true }`. Unmount calls `subscription.dispose()`, which queues `onDispose` as a microtask, then calls `invalidate(key)`, which deletes the map entry synchronously. When the microtask runs, `get(key)` returns `undefined`, and `get(key).ready` throws. The actual supplied vendored dependency was read and its SHA-256 matched the pinned manifest. Running the supplied probe with Bun from an owned after-state copy returned exit 1 with `teardown error: TypeError: undefined is not an object (evaluating 'get(key).ready')`. An owned before-state copy restoring only the removed timer returned exit 0, printed `disposed ready: true`, and completed teardown. Exact tool returns are in `raw/probe-tool-records.json`.\\n**Suggestion**: Keep immediate cache deletion, and migrate the caller's disposal contract in the same change. Retain the mounted entry directly in the disposal closure so teardown does not depend on the entry remaining discoverable through the cache. Verify that unmount immediately makes `get(key)` undefined while disposal still reports the retained entry's readiness. Merely adding optional chaining would suppress the exception while losing the required readiness value.\\n\\n## Review coverage and limits\\n\\nReviewed correctness and error propagation, temporal state management, caller/callee contract, root cause, structural integrity, verification, complexity, security, and all applicable code-quality dimensions. The missed integration of the caller with the changed lifetime contract is the same root cause as finding 1, not a separate finding. No additional concrete structural, type, boundary, file-size, complexity, or security issue was established. [critical] Immediate deletion breaks every normal unmount's deferred disposal callback\\n**Location**: outputs/working/cache/cache.mjs:4; dependent call chain outputs/working/cache/caller.mjs:5-6 and outputs/working/cache/vendor/disposer-v1.mjs:3.\\n\\n**Finding**: The new synchronous `invalidate(key)` deletes the entry before the disposal callback reads it. Calling `subscription.dispose()` first does not finish disposal: the exact vendored implementation queues the callback as a microtask. A normal unmount therefore raises an uncaught TypeError and never invokes `report`, violating the explicit requirement to preserve disposal correctness.\\n\\n**Evidence**: `mount` inserts an entry, then creates a callback evaluating `get(key).ready`. Its returned teardown first queues that callback and then deletes the map entry synchronously. After the teardown stack returns, the microtask runs, `get(key)` returns undefined, and dereferencing `ready` throws. This is deterministic ordering, with no concurrent thread or unusual input required. The pinned dependency SHA-256 matches manifest.json exactly. Running the unchanged supplied probe from an owned after-state copy with Bun 1.4.2 exits 1 and prints:\\n\\n```text\\nteardown error: TypeError: undefined is not an object (evaluating 'get(key).ready')\\n```\\n\\nA separate owned before-state copy, changing only the removed `setTimeout` line back, exits 0 and prints:\\n\\n```text\\ndisposed ready: true\\nteardown completed\\n```\\n\\nRaw commands, complete tool returns, and exit statuses are in `raw/operations.json`. No fixture implementation was substituted for the vendored dependency.\\n\\n**Suggestion**: Keep immediate cache deletion, but migrate the caller so its disposal callback retains the mounted entry directly rather than looking it up through a cache whose lifetime has ended. Capture the object passed to `put` and read that object in the callback. This removes the hidden dependency on deletion timing without adding another timer or silently dropping `report`. Verify both immediate absence from `get` and a successful deferred disposal report on the same teardown.\\n\\n## Scope and lens coverage\\n\\nReviewed the complete common prompt, diff, after-state implementation, actual dependent caller, exact locally pinned disposer, manifest and supplied probe. Correctness, state lifetime, async ordering, error propagation, root cause, integration verification and structural ownership all lead to the finding above; these are one underlying defect, not separate findings. Idempotency and same-key overlap expose broader lifetime concerns, but the supplied diff does not establish an additional independent regression. No security input-to-sink path, file-size growth, ad-hoc branching, type casts, unused compatibility layer, or unnecessary abstraction was introduced. The useful structural simplification is to remove the disposal callback's dependence on a global cache lookup; no large refactor is warranted by this fixture.\\n\\n## Limitations\\n\\nEvidence applies to this exact local vendored fixture, not a public dependency or another version. Execution used Bun 1.4.2 only. The reconstructed before-state is a comparison copy derived from the supplied diff. No fix was applied, no external service was contacted, and no other reviewer output was read. \\\\\\\"2f9b5f59a6fadcc4a00c3cf7267cfccdb57d9d9afeaf6f3fc69bdb9399379cee\\\\\\\"\\\\n}# Reviewer Prompt Template\\\\n\\\\nBuild each reviewer subagent's prompt from this template, filling in the placeholders.\\\\n\\\\n---\\\\n\\\\nYou are an adversarial code reviewer. Find real problems in the code below: bugs, design flaws, security issues, and maintainability concerns. You are not here to be helpful or encouraging. You are here to stress-test.\\\\n\\\\n## Intent\\\\n\\\\nThe author's stated intent for this change:\\\\n\\\\n> Change deferred invalidation to immediate deletion while preserving disposal correctness.\\\\n\\\\nYou are reviewing whether the code achieves this intent well. Do NOT question the intent itself. Assume the goal is correct and challenge the execution.\\\\n\\\\n## Code Under Review\\\\n\\\\n### README.md\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\nReview invalidation.diff against the after-state cache.mjs and the real caller outside the diff. The vendored disposer is the exact locally pinned dependency, not a mock pretending to prove a public dependency. Determine whether synchronous invalidation races the deferred disposal callback. probe.mjs runs this actual supplied implementation and intentionally exits nonzero if teardown throws. A before-state copy may restore the removed setTimeout line to compare ordering. No execution result is supplied.\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### manifest.json\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n{\\\\n  \\\\\\\"scope\\\\\\\": \\\\\\\"exact local vendored fixture only\\\\\\\",\\\\n  \\\\\\\"dependency\\\\\\\": {\\\\n    \\\\\\\"path\\\\\\\": \\\\\\\"vendor/disposer-v1.mjs\\\\\\\",\\\\n    \\\\\\\"version\\\\\\\": \\\\\\\"fixture-v1\\\\\\\",\\\\n    \\\\\\\"sha256\\\\\\\": \\\\\\\"d33e8d36600ad5df008657e478e39f9100a347098b4176f37923e5056cb3427f\\\\\\\"\\\\n  },\\\\n  \\\\\\\"publicPackageClaim\\\\\\\": false\\\\n}\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### cache.mjs\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\nconst entries = new Map();\\\\nexport function put(key, value) { entries.set(key, value); }\\\\nexport function get(key) { return entries.get(key); }\\\\nexport function invalidate(key) { entries.delete(key); }\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### caller.mjs\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\nimport { createDisposer } from './vendor/disposer-v1.mjs';\\\\nimport { get, put, invalidate } from './cache.mjs';\\\\nexport function mount(key, report) {\\\\n  put(key, { ready: true });\\\\n  const subscription = createDisposer(() => report(get(key).ready));\\\\n  return () => { subscription.dispose(); invalidate(key); };\\\\n}\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### invalidation.diff\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\ndiff --git a/cache.mjs b/cache.mjs\\\\n--- a/cache.mjs\\\\n+++ b/cache.mjs\\\\n@@ -1,4 +1,4 @@\\\\n const entries = new Map();\\\\n export function put(key, value) { entries.set(key, value); }\\\\n export function get(key) { return entries.get(key); }\\\\n-export function invalidate(key) { setTimeout(() => entries.delete(key), 0); }\\\\n+export function invalidate(key) { entries.delete(key); }\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### vendor/disposer-v1.mjs\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n// Exact vendored fixture dependency implementation, pinned by local SHA-256 in manifest.json.\\\\nexport function createDisposer(onDispose) {\\\\n  return { dispose: () => queueMicrotask(onDispose) };\\\\n}\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### probe.mjs\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\nimport { mount } from './caller.mjs';\\\\nlet error;\\\\nprocess.once('uncaughtException', caught => { error = caught; });\\\\nconst unmount = mount('fixture-key', ready => console.log('disposed ready:', ready));\\\\nunmount();\\\\nawait new Promise(resolve => setTimeout(resolve, 5));\\\\nconsole.log(error ? `teardown error: ${error.name}: ${error.message}` : 'teardown completed');\\\\nprocess.exitCode = error ? 1 : 0;\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n## Review Rubric\\\\n\\\\n# Review Rubric\\\\n\\\\nReview through whichever lenses are relevant. Not every lens applies to every change. Use judgment.\\\\n\\\\n## Correctness\\\\n\\\\nDoes the code actually do what the intent says it should?\\\\n\\\\n- Edge cases: empty inputs, nil/undefined, boundary values, concurrent access\\\\n- Error handling: are errors caught, propagated, or silently swallowed?\\\\n- Off-by-one, type coercion, integer overflow, string encoding\\\\n- State management: race conditions, stale closures, dangling references\\\\n- Does the happy path work? Does the sad path work?\\\\n- Idempotency: what happens if this operation runs twice, or if a previous run crashed halfway? If the answer is \\\\\\\"it depends on what state was left behind,\\\\\\\" there's a missing reconciliation step.\\\\n- Concurrency: if multiple actors can touch the same mutable state (files, branches, shared data), is access serialized structurally (locks, sequential phases, exclusive ownership), or by conventions that won't hold?\\\\n\\\\nWhen you find a potential bug, trace the execution path. Don't just flag \\\\\\\"this could be nil\\\\\\\". Show the call chain that makes it nil.\\\\n\\\\n## Root Causes vs. Symptoms\\\\n\\\\nIs the code fixing the actual problem or papering over a symptom?\\\\n\\\\nAnswering this often requires looking beyond the changed files. Read the surrounding code (callers, callees, type definitions, sibling modules) and understand the architecture the change lives in. Use the tools available to you (Read, Grep, Glob) to explore. Follow the call chain. Read the types. Understand why the code exists before judging whether the change addresses the right layer.\\\\n\\\\n- Guard clauses that mask a deeper invariant violation\\\\n- Retry logic that hides a broken contract\\\\n- Type casts that silence a modeling error\\\\n- If you see a workaround, ask: why is the workaround needed? Validate data once where it enters the system, then trust it internally.\\\\n- Abstraction level: is the code mixing high-level orchestration with low-level detail?\\\\n- Coupling: does this change introduce dependencies that will make future changes harder?\\\\n- Data model fit: do the data structures match the actual access patterns? The right structure makes downstream code obvious. The wrong one fights you at every turn.\\\\n- Bolted-on vs. integrated: was the change patched onto the existing design, or does it read as if the design always accounted for it? If the new requirement had been known from the start, would the code look like this?\\\\n- Legacy dual-paths: does the change introduce a new API while keeping the old one alive? [critical] Immediate deletion breaks every normal unmount's deferred disposal callback\\n**Location**: outputs/working/cache/cache.mjs:4; dependent call chain outputs/working/cache/caller.mjs:5-6 and outputs/working/cache/vendor/disposer-v1.mjs:3.\\n\\n**Finding**: The new synchronous `invalidate(key)` deletes the entry before the disposal callback reads it. Calling `subscription.dispose()` first does not finish disposal: the exact vendored implementation queues the callback as a microtask. A normal unmount therefore raises an uncaught TypeError and never invokes `report`, violating the explicit requirement to preserve disposal correctness.\\n\\n**Evidence**: `mount` inserts an entry, then creates a callback evaluating `get(key).ready`. Its returned teardown first queues that callback and then deletes the map entry synchronously. After the teardown stack returns, the microtask runs, `get(key)` returns undefined, and dereferencing `ready` throws. This is deterministic ordering, with no concurrent thread or unusual input required. The pinned dependency SHA-256 matches manifest.json exactly. Running the unchanged supplied probe from an owned after-state copy with Bun 1.4.2 exits 1 and prints:\\n\\n```text\\nteardown error: TypeError: undefined is not an object (evaluating 'get(key).ready')\\n```\\n\\nA separate owned before-state copy, changing only the removed `setTimeout` line back, exits 0 and prints:\\n\\n```text\\ndisposed ready: true\\nteardown completed\\n```\\n\\nRaw commands, complete tool returns, and exit statuses are in `raw/operations.json`. No fixture implementation was substituted for the vendored dependency.\\n\\n**Suggestion**: Keep immediate cache deletion, but migrate the caller so its disposal callback retains the mounted entry directly rather than looking it up through a cache whose lifetime has ended. Capture the object passed to `put` and read that object in the callback. This removes the hidden dependency on deletion timing without adding another timer or silently dropping `report`. Verify both immediate absence from `get` and a successful deferred disposal report on the same teardown.\\n\\n## Scope and lens coverage\\n\\nReviewed the complete common prompt, diff, after-state implementation, actual dependent caller, exact locally pinned disposer, manifest and supplied probe. Idempotency and same-key overlap expose broader lifetime concerns, but the supplied diff does not establish an additional independent regression. No security input-to-sink path, file-size growth, ad-hoc branching, type casts, unused compatibility layer, or unnecessary abstraction was introduced.
